in combination with the integrability of the cumulative intensities and the law of large numbers, we get
\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\Lambda_{i}(\tau,\bm{\beta}_{0})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathbb{P}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathbb{E}(\Lambda_{1}(\tau,\bm{\beta}_{0}))
, as
n\rightarrow\infty
. Hence, the whole expression
converges to zero in probability, as
n\rightarrow\infty
, and we conclude that (
I.32
) holds.

The subsequent considerations relate to the second term of the second step of (
I.31
). According to
I.2.1

(ii)
it holds that
\sup_{t\in\mathcal{T}}\lVert\tilde{\textbf{h}}_{1}(t,\bm{\beta}_{0})\rVert_{\infty}
is bounded. Moreover, we have
\mathbb{E}(\Lambda_{1}(\tau,\bm{\beta}_{0}))<\infty
by assumption. These two statements combined yield for all
t\in\mathcal{T}
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\Big(\int_{0}^{t}\lVert\tilde{\textbf{h}}_{1}(u,\bm{\beta}_{0})^{\otimes 2}\rVert_{\infty}\,d\Lambda_{1}(u,\bm{\beta}_{0})\Big)\leq\mathbb{E}\Big(\sup_{t\in\mathcal{T}}\lVert\tilde{\textbf{h}}_{1}(t,\bm{\beta}_{0})^{\otimes 2}\rVert_{\infty}\Lambda_{1}(t,\bm{\beta}_{0})\Big)<\infty.

(I.34)

On the basis of (
I.34
) and
I.2.1

(iii)
, we make use of the law of large numbers and get for the second term of the second step of (
I.31
)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\int_{0}^{t}\tilde{\textbf{h}}_{i}(u,\bm{\beta}_{0})^{\otimes 2}d\Lambda_{i}(u,\bm{\beta}_{0})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathbb{P}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathbb{E}(\int_{0}^{t}\tilde{\textbf{h}}_{1}(u,\bm{\beta}_{0})^{\otimes 2}d\Lambda_{1}(u,\bm{\beta}_{0})),\quad n\rightarrow\infty,

for any fixed
t\in\mathcal{T}
. Note that the integrability of the intensity process
\lambda_{1}(t,\bm{\beta}_{0})
follows from the integrability of the cumulative intensity process
\Lambda_{1}(t,\bm{\beta}_{0})
. Thus,
due to the integrability of the cumulative intensities and

I.2.1

(ii)
, we can make use of Fubini’s theorem, due to which we can exchange the order of integration. Thus, we have

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\int_{0}^{t}\tilde{\textbf{h}}_{i}(u,\bm{\beta}_{0})^{\otimes 2}d\Lambda_{i}(u,\bm{\beta}_{0})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathbb{P}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\int_{0}^{t}\mathbb{E}(\tilde{\textbf{h}}_{1}(u,\bm{\beta}_{0})^{\otimes 2}\lambda_{1}(u,\bm{\beta}_{0}))du,

(I.35)

for all
t\in\mathcal{T}
, as
n\rightarrow\infty
. Finally, combining (
I.31
) with (
I.32
) and (
I.35
) yields

\displaystyle\langle\textbf{D}_{n,h}\rangle(t)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathbb{P}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\int_{0}^{t}\mathbb{E}(\tilde{\textbf{h}}_{1}(u,\bm{\beta}_{0})^{\otimes 2}\lambda_{1}(u,\bm{\beta}_{0}))du=\textbf{V}_{\tilde{h}}(t),\text{ for all }t\in\mathcal{T},\text{ as }n\rightarrow\infty.

When taking into consideration that we have
\tilde{\textbf{h}}=(\tilde{\textbf{k}},\tilde{\textbf{g}})
, we can write the covariance matrix in block form

\textbf{V}_{\tilde{h}}=\textbf{V}_{(\tilde{k},\tilde{g})}=\begin{pmatrix}\textbf{V}_{\tilde{k}}&\textbf{V}_{\tilde{k},\tilde{g}}\\
\textbf{V}_{\tilde{g},\tilde{k}}&\textbf{V}_{\tilde{g}}\end{pmatrix},

where for
t\in\mathcal{T}
,

\textbf{V}_{\tilde{k}}(t)=\langle\textbf{D}_{\tilde{k}}\rangle(t)=\int_{0}^{t}\mathbb{E}(\tilde{\textbf{k}}_{1}(u,\bm{\beta}_{0})^{\otimes 2}\lambda_{1}(u,\bm{\beta}_{0}))du,

\textbf{V}_{\tilde{g}}(t)=\langle\textbf{D}_{\tilde{g}}\rangle(t)=\int_{0}^{t}\mathbb{E}(\tilde{\textbf{g}}_{1}(u,\bm{\beta}_{0})^{\otimes 2}\lambda_{1}(u,\bm{\beta}_{0}))du,

\textbf{V}_{\tilde{k},\tilde{g}}(t)=\textbf{V}_{\tilde{g},\tilde{k}}(t)=\langle\textbf{D}_{\tilde{k}},\textbf{D}_{\tilde{g}}\rangle(t)=\int_{0}^{t}\mathbb{E}(\tilde{\textbf{k}}_{1}(u,\bm{\beta}_{0})\cdot\tilde{\textbf{g}}_{1}(u,\bm{\beta}_{0})^{\top}\lambda_{1}(u,\bm{\beta}_{0}))du.

Second, we verify condition (2.5.3) of Rebolledo’s theorem of
Andersen et al. 1993
. For this we introduce the stochastic process
\textbf{D}_{n,h}^{\epsilon}
given by

\displaystyle\textbf{D}_{n,h}^{\epsilon}(t)

\displaystyle{}=\int_{0}^{t}\mathbbm{1}\{|\Delta\textbf{D}_{n,h}(u)\rvert>\epsilon\}\textbf{D}_{n,h}(du),\quad t\in\mathcal{T},

(I.36)

which we refer to as the
\epsilon
-jump process of
\textbf{D}_{n,h}
. Here, the indicator function is to be understood vector-wise, specifying for each element
D_{n,h}^{j}(t)
of the
p
-dimensional vector
\textbf{D}_{n,h}(t)=(D_{n,h}^{1}(t),\ldots,D_{n,h}^{p}(t))
whether the jump at time t is larger in absolute value than
\epsilon
. Note that the elements of the indicator function
\mathbbm{1}\{|\Delta\textbf{D}_{n,h}(u)\rvert\geq\epsilon\}
may be unequal to zero only at discontinuities of
D_{n,h}^{j}
, which correspond to discontinuities of the martingale
M_{i}
. In addition, the jumps of the martingale
M_{i}
occur only at event times registered by the counting processes
N_{i}
, because we assumed the cumulative intensity process
\Lambda_{i}(\cdot,\bm{\beta}_{0})
to be absolutely continuous. This means that the
\epsilon
-jump process
\textbf{D}_{n,h}^{\epsilon}
accumulates all the jumps of components of
\textbf{D}_{n,h}
that are larger in absolute value than
\epsilon
. Recall that no two counting processes
N_{i}
,
i=1,\ldots,n,
jump simultaneously. Combining (
I.36
) with the above reasoning yields

\displaystyle\textbf{D}_{n,h}^{\epsilon}

\displaystyle{}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\int_{0}^{t}\textbf{h}_{n,i}(u,\bm{\beta}_{0})\mathbbm{1}\Big\{\Big|\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\textbf{h}_{n,i}(u,\bm{\beta}_{0})\Delta M_{i}(u)\Big\rvert>\epsilon\Big\}dM_{i}(u),

\displaystyle{}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\int_{0}^{t}\textbf{h}_{n,i}(u,\bm{\beta}_{0})\mathbbm{1}\Big\{\Big|\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\textbf{h}_{n,i}(u,\bm{\beta}_{0})\Delta N_{i}(u)\Big\rvert>\epsilon\Big\}dM_{i}(u).

The aforementioned condition (2.5.3) is fulfilled, if the predictable covariation process
\langle\textbf{D}_{n,h}^{\epsilon}\rangle(t)
of
\textbf{D}_{n,h}^{\epsilon}
converges to zero in probability for all
t\in\mathcal{T},\epsilon>0
, as
n\rightarrow\infty
.
Note that the predictable covariation process
\langle\textbf{D}_{n,h}^{\epsilon}\rangle(t)
is defined as the
(p+b)\times(p+b)
-dimensional matrix of the predictable covariation processes
\big(\langle D_{n,h}^{\epsilon,j},D_{n,h}^{\epsilon,l}\rangle(t)\big)_{j,l=1}^{p+b}
of the components

D_{n,h}^{\epsilon,j}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\int_{0}^{t}h^{j}_{n,i}(u,\bm{\beta}_{0})\mathbbm{1}\Big\{\Big|\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}h_{n,i}^{j}(u,\bm{\beta}_{0})\Delta N_{i}(u)\Big\rvert>\epsilon\Big\}dM_{i}(u),

where
{h}_{n,i}^{j}
denotes the
j
-th component of the
(p+b)
-dimensional function
\textbf{h}_{n,i}
,
j=1,\ldots,p+b
. It is easy to see that the largest entry (in absolute value) of
\langle\textbf{D}_{n,h}^{\epsilon}\rangle(t)
is located on the diagonal and that a diagonal element takes the following form:

\displaystyle\langle D_{n,h}^{\epsilon,j}\rangle(t)=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\int_{0}^{t}h_{n,i}^{j}(u,\bm{\beta}_{0})^{2}\mathbbm{1}\Big\{\Big|\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}h_{n,i}^{j}(u,\bm{\beta}_{0})\Delta N_{i}(u)\Big|>\epsilon\Big\}d\Lambda_{i}(u),

j=1,\ldots,p+b
. Thus, it suffices to show that the diagonal elements
\langle D_{n,h}^{\epsilon,j}\rangle(t)
of
\langle\textbf{D}_{n,h}^{\epsilon}\rangle(t)
converge to 0 in probability as
n\to\infty
for each
t\in\mathcal{T}
,
j=1,\dots,p+b
. That is, for every
\delta>0
the probability
\mathbb{P}(\langle D_{n,h}^{\epsilon,j}\rangle(t)\geq\delta)
must go to zero for all
j=1,\dots,p+b
.
For this, we bound this probability from above as follows:

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\mathbb{P}(\langle D_{n,h}^{\epsilon,j}\rangle(t)\geq\delta)\\
&\leq\mathbb{P}\Big(\sup_{t\in\mathcal{T},i\in\{1,\ldots,n\}}\mathbbm{1}\{\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\lVert\textbf{h}_{n,i}(t,\bm{\beta}_{0})\rVert_{\infty}>\epsilon\}\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\int_{0}^{t}h_{n,i}^{j}(u,\bm{\beta}_{0})^{2}d\Lambda_{i}(u)\geq\delta\Big)\\
&\leq\mathbb{P}\Big(\sup_{t\in\mathcal{T},i\in\{1,\ldots,n\}}\mathbbm{1}\{\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\lVert\textbf{h}_{n,i}(t,\bm{\beta}_{0})\rVert_{\infty}>\epsilon\}=1\Big)\\
&=1-\mathbb{P}\Big(\text{for all }i,t:\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\lVert\textbf{h}_{n,i}(t,\bm{\beta}_{0})\rVert_{\infty}\leq\epsilon\Big)\\
&=o(1)+1-\mathbb{P}\Big(\text{for all }i,t:\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\lVert\textbf{h}_{n,i}(t,\bm{\beta}_{0})\rVert_{\infty}\leq\epsilon,\\
&\qquad\sup_{i\in\{1,\ldots,n\},t\in\mathcal{T}}\lVert\textbf{h}_{n,i}(t,\bm{\beta}_{0})-\tilde{\textbf{h}}_{i}(t,\bm{\beta}_{0})\rVert_{\infty}<\eta\Big)\\
&\leq o(1)+1-\mathbb{P}\Big(\text{for all }i,t:\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\lVert\tilde{\textbf{h}}_{i}(t,\bm{\beta}_{0})\rVert_{\infty}+\frac{\eta}{\sqrt{n}}<\epsilon\Big).\end{split}

(I.37)

where the one but last equality of (
I.37
)
is due to
I.2.1

(i)
and holds for any
\eta>0
.
The inequality in the last line of (
I.37
)
was obtained by adding and subtracting
\tilde{\textbf{h}}_{i}(t,\bm{\beta}_{0})
to the norm two lines above it,
namely by writing
\lVert\textbf{h}_{n,i}(t,\bm{\beta}_{0})\rVert_{\infty}=\lVert\textbf{h}_{n,i}(t,\bm{\beta}_{0})-\tilde{\textbf{h}}_{i}(t,\bm{\beta}_{0})+\tilde{\textbf{h}}_{i}(t,\bm{\beta}_{0})\rVert_{\infty}
.

Under
I.2.1

(ii)
the probability
\mathbb{P}(\text{for all }i,t:\ {\displaystyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\lVert\tilde{\textbf{h}}_{i}(t,\bm{\beta}_{0})\rVert_{\infty}+\frac{\eta}{\sqrt{n}}}<\epsilon)
converges to one and, hence, the initial probability
\mathbb{P}(\langle D_{n,h}^{\epsilon,j}\rangle(t)\geq\delta)
to zero as
n\to\infty
for each
t\in\mathcal{T}
and across all components
j=1,\dots,d
. Thus, condition (2.5.3) of Rebolledo’s theorem as stated in Theorem II.5.1 of
Andersen et al. 1993
is fulfilled. In conclusion, both requirements of Rebolledo’s theorem have been verified and the proof of
I.2.2
is complete.
\blacksquare

Proof of
I.2.4
.

We wish to show that

\sup_{t\in\mathcal{T}}\lVert\textbf{B}_{n}(t)-\textbf{B}(t)\rVert\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathbb{P}}}{{\longrightarrow}}0,\text{ as }n\rightarrow\infty,

where
\textbf{B}_{n}(t)=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\int_{0}^{t}\textnormal{D}\textbf{k}_{n,i}(u,\bm{\beta}_{0})dN_{i}(u)
and
\textbf{B}(t)=\int_{0}^{t}\mathbb{E}(\tilde{\textbf{K}}_{1}(u,\bm{\beta}_{0})\lambda_{1}(u,\bm{\beta}_{0}))du
,
t\in\mathcal{T}
. For this we point out that the compensator of
\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}N_{i}(\tau)
is equal to
\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\Lambda_{i}(\tau,\bm{\beta}_{0})
. From the integrability of the cumulative intensities,
I.2.3

(iii)
, and the law of large numbers, we can conclude that
\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\Lambda_{i}(\tau,\bm{\beta}_{0})=O_{p}(1)
. Thus, we get from Lenglart’s inequality that
\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}N_{i}(\tau)=O_{p}(1)
. Combining this argument with
I.2.3

(i)
yields

\displaystyle\begin{split}&{}\sup_{t\in\mathcal{T}}\lVert\textbf{B}_{n}(t)-\textbf{B}(t)\rVert\\
&{}\leq\sup_{t\in\mathcal{T}}\Big\lVert\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\int_{0}^{t}[\textnormal{D}\textbf{k}_{n,i}(u,\bm{\beta}_{0})-\tilde{\textbf{K}}_{i}(u,\bm{\beta}_{0})]dN_{i}(u)\Big\rVert\\
&{}\quad+\sup_{t\in\mathcal{T}}\Big\lVert\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\int_{0}^{t}\tilde{\textbf{K}}_{i}(u,\bm{\beta}_{0})dN_{i}(u)-\int_{0}^{t}\mathbb{E}(\tilde{\textbf{K}}_{1}(u,\bm{\beta}_{0})\lambda_{1}(u,\bm{\beta}_{0}))du\Big\rVert\\
&{}\leq\sup_{t\in\mathcal{T}}\Big\lVert\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\int_{0}^{t}\tilde{\textbf{K}}_{i}(u,\bm{\beta}_{0})dM_{i}(u)\Big\rVert\\
&{}\quad+\sup_{t\in\mathcal{T}}\Big\lVert\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\int_{0}^{t}\tilde{\textbf{K}}_{i}(u,\bm{\beta}_{0})d\Lambda_{i}(u,\bm{\beta}_{0})-\int_{0}^{t}\mathbb{E}(\tilde{\textbf{K}}_{1}(u,\bm{\beta}_{0})\lambda_{1}(u,\bm{\beta}_{0}))du\Big\rVert+o_{p}(1),\end{split}

(I.38)

where in the last step the Doob-Meyer decomposition (
I.3
) has been applied.
With
I.2.3

(ii)
and Proposition II.4.1. of
Andersen et al. 1993
it follows that the integral
\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\int_{0}^{t}\tilde{\textbf{K}}_{i}(u,\bm{\beta}_{0})dM_{i}(u)
is a local square integrable martingale. The elements of the corresponding predictable covariation process at
\tau
can be bounded from above by

\frac{1}{n^{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\int_{0}^{\tau}\lVert\tilde{\textbf{K}}_{i}(u,\bm{\beta}_{0})\rVert^{2}_{\infty}d\Lambda_{i}(u,\bm{\beta}_{0}).

According to
I.2.3

(ii)
,
\sup_{i\in\{1,\ldots,n\},t\in\mathcal{T}}\lVert\tilde{\textbf{K}}_{i}(t,\bm{\beta}_{0})\rVert^{2}_{\infty}
is bounded for
i\in\mathbb{N}
, and, as stated above, it holds
\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\Lambda_{i}(\tau,\bm{\beta}_{0})=O_{p}(1)
. Hence, the considered predictable covariation process and further, according to Lenglart’s inequality, the first term of the second step on the right-hand side of (
I.38
) converges to zero in probability, as
n\rightarrow\infty
.
It is only left to show that

\displaystyle\sup_{t\in\mathcal{T}}\Big\lVert\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\int_{0}^{t}\tilde{\textbf{K}}_{i}(u,\bm{\beta}_{0})d\Lambda_{i}(u,\bm{\beta}_{0})-\int_{0}^{t}\mathbb{E}(\tilde{\textbf{K}}_{1}(u,\bm{\beta}_{0})\lambda_{1}(u,\bm{\beta}_{0}))du\Big\rVert=o_{p}(1),

(I.39)

as
n\rightarrow\infty
. According to the integrability of the cumulative intensities and
I.2.3

(ii)
it follows that
\mathbb{E}(\int_{0}^{t}\lVert\tilde{\textbf{K}}_{1}(u,\bm{\beta}_{0})\rVert_{\infty}\lambda_{1}(u,\bm{\beta}_{0})du)<\infty
. From this argument in combination with
I.2.3

(iii)
and the law of large numbers, we have that

\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\int_{0}^{t}\tilde{\textbf{K}}_{i}(u,\bm{\beta}_{0})\lambda_{i}(u,\bm{\beta}_{0})du
converges almost surely to
\mathbb{E}(\int_{0}^{t}\tilde{\textbf{K}}_{1}(u,\bm{\beta}_{0})\lambda_{1}(u,\bm{\beta}_{0})du)

for any fixed
t\in\mathcal{T}
, as
n\rightarrow\infty
. Note that the integrability of the intensity process
\lambda_{1}(t,\bm{\beta}_{0})
follows from the integrability of the cumulative intensity process
\Lambda_{1}(t,\bm{\beta}_{0})
. Thus,
due to the integrability of the cumulative intensities and

I.2.3

(ii)
, we can make use of Fubini’s theorem, by which we can exchange the order of integration. We can conclude that

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\int_{0}^{t}\tilde{\textbf{K}}_{i}(u,\bm{\beta}_{0})d\Lambda_{i}(u,\bm{\beta}_{0})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\mathbb{P}}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\int_{0}^{t}\mathbb{E}(\tilde{\textbf{K}}_{1}(u,\bm{\beta}_{0})\lambda_{1}(u,\bm{\beta}_{0}))du,

(I.40)

pointwise in
t\in\mathcal{T}
, as
n\rightarrow\infty
.

Next, we show the corresponding uniform convergence in probability on
\mathcal{T}
. For this, we divide the interval
\mathcal{T}=[0,\tau]
into
N
equidistant subintervals
[t_{l},t_{l+1}]
with
t_{0}=0
,
t_{N}=\tau
, and
l\in\{0,1,\ldots,N-1\}
.
The width of a subinterval is chosen such that

\int_{t_{l}}^{t_{l+1}}\mathbb{E}(\lVert\tilde{\textbf{K}}_{1}(u,\bm{\beta}_{0})\rVert\lambda(u,\bm{\beta}_{0}))du\leq\delta/2

for all
l\in\{0,1,\ldots,N-1\}
. For
t\in[0,\tau)
we denote the lower and upper endpoint of the subinterval containing
t
by
t_{l(t)}=\max_{l\in\{0,1,\ldots,N-1\}}\{t_{l}:t_{l}\leq t\}
and
t_{l(t)+1}=\min_{l\in\{1,\ldots,N\}}\{t_{l}:t_{l}>t\}
, respectively. For
t=\tau
we choose
t_{l(\tau)}=t_{l(\tau)+1}=\tau
. In the following derivation we make use of (
I.40
) and get

\displaystyle{}\sup_{t\in\mathcal{T}}\Big\lVert\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\int_{0}^{t}\tilde{\textbf{K}}_{i}(u,\bm{\beta}_{0})\lambda_{i}(u,\bm{\beta}_{0})du-\int_{0}^{t}\mathbb{E}(\tilde{\textbf{K}}_{1}(u,\bm{\beta}_{0})\lambda_{1}(u,\bm{\beta}_{0}))du\Big\lVert

\displaystyle{}=\sup_{t\in\mathcal{T}}\Big\lVert\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\int_{0}^{t}\tilde{\textbf{K}}_{i}(u,\bm{\beta}_{0})\lambda_{i}(u,\bm{\beta}_{0})du-\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\int_{0}^{t_{l(t)}}\tilde{\textbf{K}}_{i}(u,\bm{\beta}_{0})\lambda_{i}(u,\bm{\beta}_{0})du

\displaystyle{}\quad+\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\int_{0}^{t_{l(t)}}\tilde{\textbf{K}}_{i}(u,\bm{\beta}_{0})\lambda_{i}(u,\bm{\beta}_{0})du-\int_{0}^{t_{l(t)}}\mathbb{E}(\tilde{\textbf{K}}_{1}(u,\bm{\beta}_{0})\lambda_{1}(u,\bm{\beta}_{0}))du

\displaystyle{}\quad+\int_{0}^{t_{l(t)}}\mathbb{E}(\tilde{\textbf{K}}_{1}(u,\bm{\beta}_{0})\lambda_{1}(u,\bm{\beta}_{0}))du-\int_{0}^{t}\mathbb{E}(\tilde{\textbf{K}}_{1}(u,\bm{\beta}_{0})\lambda_{1}(u,\bm{\beta}_{0}))du\Big\lVert

\displaystyle{}\leq\sup_{t\in\mathcal{T}}\Big(\Big\lVert\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\int_{t_{l(t)}}^{t}\tilde{\textbf{K}}_{i}(u,\bm{\beta}_{0})\lambda_{i}(u,\bm{\beta}_{0})du-\int_{t_{l(t)}}^{t}\mathbb{E}(\tilde{\textbf{K}}_{1}(u,\bm{\beta}_{0})\lambda_{1}(u,\bm{\beta}_{0}))du\Big\lVert\Big)+o_{p}(1)

\displaystyle{}\leq\sup_{t\in\mathcal{T}}\Big(\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\int_{t_{l(t)}}^{t}\lVert\tilde{\textbf{K}}_{i}(u,\bm{\beta}_{0})\rVert\lambda_{i}(u,\bm{\beta}_{0})du+\int_{t_{l(t)}}^{t}\mathbb{E}(\lVert\tilde{\textbf{K}}_{1}(u,\bm{\beta}_{0})\lVert\lambda_{1}(u,\bm{\beta}_{0}))du\Big)+o_{p}(1)

\displaystyle{}\leq\max_{l\in\{0,\ldots,N-1\}}\Big(\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\int_{t_{l}}^{t_{l+1}}\lVert\tilde{\textbf{K}}_{i}(u,\bm{\beta}_{0})\rVert\lambda_{i}(u,\bm{\beta}_{0})du

\displaystyle{}\quad+\int_{t_{l}}^{t_{l+1}}\mathbb{E}(\lVert\tilde{\textbf{K}}_{1}(u,\bm{\beta}_{0})\lVert\lambda_{1}(u,\bm{\beta}_{0}))du\Big)+o_{p}(1)

\displaystyle{}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathbb{P}}}{{\longrightarrow}}2\cdot\max_{l\in\{0,\ldots,N-1\}}\Big(\int_{t_{l}}^{t_{l+1}}\mathbb{E}(\lVert\tilde{\textbf{K}}_{1}(u,\bm{\beta}_{0})\lVert\lambda_{1}(u,\bm{\beta}_{0}))du\Big)\leq\delta,\quad n\rightarrow\infty.

The convergence involved in the last step of the considerations above, follows from the same arguments that led to (
I.40
). As we can choose the length of the subintervals
[t_{l},t_{l+1}]
such that
\delta>0
is arbitrarily small, we obtain (
I.39
).
\blacksquare

Proof of
I.2.6
.

We aim to derive the limit in law of
\textbf{D}_{n,k}+\textbf{B}_{n}\textbf{C}_{n}\textbf{D}_{n,g}(\tau)
, as
n\rightarrow\infty
, where
\textbf{D}_{n,k}
and
\textbf{D}_{n,g}
are vector-valued local square integrable martingales,
\textbf{B}_{n}
is a matrix-valued stochastic process and
\textbf{C}_{n}
is a random matrix.
For this, we first show that the weak limit of
(\textbf{D}_{n,k}^{\top},\textbf{D}_{n,g}^{\top},\text{vec}(\textbf{B}_{n})^{\top},\text{vec}(\textbf{C}_{n})^{\top})
is
(\textbf{D}_{k}^{\top},\textbf{D}_{g}^{\top},\text{vec}(\textbf{B})^{\top},\text{vec}(\textbf{C})^{\top})
, as
n\rightarrow\infty
.
According to
I.2.2
, we have

(\textbf{D}_{n,k}^{\top},\textbf{D}_{n,g}^{\top})^{\top}=\textbf{D}_{n,h}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{L}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\textbf{D}_{\tilde{h}}=(\textbf{D}_{\tilde{k}}^{\top},\textbf{D}_{\tilde{g}}^{\top})^{\top},\quad\text{in}\;(D(\mathcal{T}))^{p+b},\text{ as }n\rightarrow\infty,

where
\textbf{D}_{\tilde{h}}
is a continuous zero-mean Gaussian
(p+b)
-dimensional vector martingale with covariance function
\textbf{V}_{\tilde{h}}(t)=\int_{0}^{t}\mathbb{E}(\tilde{\textbf{h}}_{1}(u,\bm{\beta}_{0})^{\otimes 2}\lambda_{1}(u,\bm{\beta}_{0}))du
,
t\in\mathcal{T}
.
As
\textbf{D}_{\tilde{h}}\in\mathcal{C}[0,\tau]^{p+b}
, we know that
\textbf{D}_{\tilde{h}}
is separable. Furthermore, we have shown in
I.2.4
that there exists a
p\times q
-dimensional continuous, deterministic function
\textbf{B}(t)
,
t\in\mathcal{T}
, such that
\sup_{t\in\mathcal{T}}\lVert\textbf{B}_{n}(t)-\textbf{B}(t)\rVert\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathbb{P}}}{{\longrightarrow}}0
, as
n\rightarrow\infty
. In other words, the limit in law
\text{vec}(\textbf{B})
of
\text{vec}(\textbf{B}_{n})
is a constant of the space
\mathcal{C}[0,\tau]^{pq}
. Thus, we conclude with Example 1.4.7 of
van der Vaart and Wellner 1996
that

(\textbf{D}_{n,h}^{\top},\text{vec}(\textbf{B}_{n})^{\top})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{L}}}{{\longrightarrow}}(\textbf{D}_{\tilde{h}}^{\top},\text{vec}(\textbf{B})^{\top}),\text{ in }D[0,\tau]^{p+b+pq},\text{ as }n\rightarrow\infty.

As the last step of the first part of this proof we argue that

\displaystyle(\textbf{D}_{n,h}^{\top},\text{vec}(\textbf{B}_{n})^{\top},\text{vec}(\textbf{C}_{n})^{\top})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{L}}}{{\longrightarrow}}(\textbf{D}_{\tilde{h}}(t)^{\top},\text{vec}(\textbf{B})^{\top},\text{vec}(\textbf{C})^{\top}),

(I.41)

in
\mathcal{D}[0,\tau]^{p+b+pq}\times\mathbb{R}^{pq}
, as
n\rightarrow\infty
. For this, we point out that
(\textbf{D}_{\tilde{h}}^{\top},\text{vec}(\textbf{B})^{\top})\in\mathcal{C}[0,\tau]^{p+b+pq}
. Thus,
(\textbf{D}_{\tilde{h}}^{\top},\text{vec}(\textbf{B})^{\top})
is separable. Additionally, we have assumed in
I.2.5
that the random
q\times p
-dimensional matrix
\textbf{C}_{n}
converges in probability to the deterministic matrix
C
, as
n\rightarrow\infty
. Because
\textbf{C}_{n}
is asymptotically degenerate and
(\textbf{D}_{\tilde{h}}^{\top},\text{vec}(\textbf{B})^{\top})
is separable, we again use Example 1.4.7 of
van der Vaart and Wellner 1996
and infer that (
I.41
) holds.

It only remains to apply the continuous mapping theorem to (
I.41
) in order to derive the weak limit of
\textbf{D}_{n,k}+\textbf{B}_{n}\textbf{C}_{n}\textbf{D}_{n,g}
, as
n\rightarrow\infty
. In particular, we use the following three maps

\begin{array}[]{r@{}l}&{}f_{1}:(\textbf{D}_{n,k}^{\top},\textbf{D}_{n,g}(\tau)^{\top},\text{vec}(\textbf{B}_{n})^{\top},\text{vec}(\textbf{C}_{n})^{\top})\mapsto(\textbf{D}_{n,k}^{\top},\textbf{D}_{n,g}(\tau)^{\top},\text{vec}(\textbf{B}_{n}\textbf{C}_{n})^{\top})\\
&{}f_{2}:(\textbf{D}_{n,k}^{\top},\textbf{D}_{n,g}(\tau)^{\top},\text{vec}(\textbf{B}_{n}\textbf{C}_{n})^{\top})\mapsto(\textbf{D}_{n,k}^{\top},(\textbf{B}_{n}\textbf{C}_{n}\textbf{D}_{n,g}(\tau))^{\top})\\
&{}f_{3}:(\textbf{D}_{n,k}^{\top},(\textbf{B}_{n}\textbf{C}_{n}\textbf{D}_{n,g}(\tau))^{\top})\mapsto(\textbf{D}_{n,k}+\textbf{B}_{n}\textbf{C}_{n}\textbf{D}_{n,g}(\tau)).\\
\end{array}

Recall that
(\textbf{D}_{\tilde{k}}^{\top},\textbf{D}_{\tilde{g}}^{\top},\text{vec}(\textbf{B})^{\top},\text{vec}(\textbf{C})^{\top})\in\mathcal{C}[0,\tau]^{p+b+2pq}
. Thus, it follows successively with the continuous mapping theorem and the maps
f_{1},f_{2}
and
f_{3}
that

\textbf{D}_{n,k}+\textbf{B}_{n}\textbf{C}_{n}\textbf{D}_{n,g}(\tau)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{L}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\textbf{D}_{\tilde{k}}+\textbf{B}\textbf{C}\textbf{D}_{\tilde{g}}(\tau)\text{ in }D[0,\tau]^{p},

as
n\rightarrow\infty
.
Moreover, the covariance function of
\textbf{D}_{\tilde{k}}+\textbf{B}\textbf{C}\textbf{D}_{\tilde{g}}(\tau)
at
t\in\mathcal{T}
maps
t
to

\displaystyle{}\textbf{V}_{\tilde{k}}(t)+\textbf{B}(t)\textbf{C}\textbf{V}_{\tilde{g}}(\tau)\textbf{C}^{\top}\textbf{B}(t)^{\top}+[\textbf{V}_{\tilde{k},\tilde{g}}(t)+\text{Cov}(\textbf{D}_{\tilde{k}}(t),\textbf{D}_{\tilde{g}}(\tau)-\textbf{D}_{\tilde{g}}(t))]\textbf{C}^{\top}\textbf{B}(t)^{\top}

\displaystyle{}\quad+\textbf{B}(t)\textbf{C}[\textbf{V}_{\tilde{g},\tilde{k}}(t)+\text{Cov}(\textbf{D}_{\tilde{g}}(\tau)-\textbf{D}_{\tilde{g}}(t),\textbf{D}_{\tilde{k}}(t))]

\displaystyle={}\textbf{V}_{\tilde{k}}(t)+\textbf{B}(t)\textbf{C}\textbf{V}_{\tilde{g}}(\tau)\textbf{C}^{\top}\textbf{B}(t)^{\top}+\textbf{V}_{\tilde{k},\tilde{g}}(t)\textbf{C}^{\top}\textbf{B}(t)^{\top}+\textbf{B}(t)\textbf{C}\textbf{V}_{\tilde{g},\tilde{k}}(t),

where
\text{Cov}(\textbf{D}_{\tilde{k}}(t),\textbf{D}_{\tilde{g}}(\tau)-\textbf{D}_{\tilde{g}}(t))=\text{Cov}(\textbf{D}_{\tilde{g}}(\tau)-\textbf{D}_{\tilde{g}}(t),\textbf{D}_{\tilde{k}}(t))^{\top}=0
, because

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}(\textbf{D}_{\tilde{k}}(t)(\textbf{D}_{\tilde{g}}(\tau)-\textbf{D}_{\tilde{g}}(t))^{\top})

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}(\mathbb{E}(\textbf{D}_{\tilde{k}}(t)(\textbf{D}_{\tilde{g}}(\tau)-\textbf{D}_{\tilde{g}}(t))^{\top}|\mathcal{F}_{1}(t)))

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}(\textbf{D}_{\tilde{k}}(t)\mathbb{E}((\textbf{D}_{\tilde{g}}(\tau)-\textbf{D}_{\tilde{g}}(t))^{\top}))

\displaystyle=0.

Here the one but last step holds because
\sigma(\textbf{D}_{\tilde{k}}(t))\in\mathcal{F}_{1}(t)
and
\textbf{D}_{\tilde{g}}(\tau)-\textbf{D}_{\tilde{g}}(t)
is independent of
\mathcal{F}_{1}(t)
. In the last step it has been applied that
\mathbb{E}(\textbf{D}_{\tilde{g}}(\tau)-\textbf{D}_{\tilde{g}}(t))=0
.

\blacksquare

A.2 Proofs of
Section

I.3

Proof of
I.3.2
.

In the first part of this proof, we show that, conditionally on the initial
\sigma
-algebra
\mathcal{F}_{2}(0)
, the stochastic process
\textbf{D}^{*}_{n,h}(t)=({D}^{*,1}_{n,h}(t),\ldots,{D}^{*,p+b}_{n,h}(t))
,
t\in\mathcal{T}
, is a
(p+b)
-dimensional vector of square integrable martingales with respect to
\mathcal{F}_{2}(t)
.
Here, the j-th element
{D}^{*,j}_{n,h}
of
\textbf{D}^{*}_{n,h}
,
j=1,\ldots,p+b
, is given by

{D}^{*,j}_{n,h}(t)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\int_{0}^{t}{h}^{j}_{n,i}(u,\hat{\bm{\beta}}_{n})G_{i}(u)\,dN_{i}(u),\quad t\in\mathcal{T},

where
{h}^{j}_{n,i}(t,\hat{\bm{\beta}}_{n})
denotes the j-th element of the
(p+b)
-dimensional function
\textbf{h}_{n,i}(t,\hat{\bm{\beta}}_{n})
.
For later use we write
{D}^{*,j}_{n,h}
as the scaled sum over
{D}^{*,j}_{n,h,i}=\int_{0}^{\cdot}{h}^{j}_{n,i}(u,\hat{\bm{\beta}}_{n})G_{i}(u)\,dN_{i}(u)
, namely
{D}^{*,j}_{n,h}(t)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}{D}^{*,j}_{n,h,i}(t)
,
t\in\mathcal{T}
. Furthermore, by incorporating the jump time points
T_{i,1},\ldots,T_{i,n_{i}}
of the counting process
N_{i}
, we can write

{D}^{*,j}_{n,h}(t)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{r:T_{i,r}\leq t}{h}^{j}_{n,i}(T_{i,r},\hat{\bm{\beta}})G_{i}(T_{i,r}),\quad t\in\mathcal{T}.

Clearly, all stochastic processes
{D}^{*,j}_{n,h}(t)
,
t\in\mathcal{T}
,
j=1,\ldots,p+b
, are adapted to the filtration
\mathcal{F}_{2}(t)
,
t\in\mathcal{T}
. Moreover, for all
j=1,\ldots,p+b
,
{D}^{*,j}_{n,h}
is cadlag, as the same holds for the counting processes
N_{i}
,
i=1,\ldots,n
. As we work with a probability space, square integrability implies integrability of a stochastic process.
Thus, we directly show that
{D}^{*,j}_{n,h}
is square integrable for all
j=1,\ldots,p+b
. For this we wish to show that

\sup_{t\in\mathcal{T}}\mathbb{E}_{0}(D^{*,j}_{n,h}(t)^{2})=\sup_{t\in\mathcal{T}}\mathbb{E}_{0}\Big(\frac{1}{n}\Big(\sum_{i=1}^{n}D^{*,j}_{n,h,i}(t)\Big)^{2}\Big)<\infty,

where
\mathbb{E}_{0}
denotes the conditional expectation
\mathbb{E}(\cdot|\mathcal{F}_{2}(0))
. In preparation for this, we state

\displaystyle\begin{split}&{}\frac{1}{n}\Big(\sum_{i=1}^{n}D^{*,j}_{n,h,i}(t)\Big)^{2}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{l=1}^{n}D^{*,j}_{n,h,i}(t)D^{*,j}_{n,h,l}(t)\\
&{}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{l=1}^{n}\sum_{r:T_{i,r}\leq t}\sum_{v:T_{i,v}\leq t}{h}^{j}_{n,i}(T_{i,r},\hat{\bm{\beta}}_{n}){h}^{j}_{n,l}(T_{l,v},\hat{\bm{\beta}}_{n})G_{i}(T_{i,r})G_{l}(T_{l,v}).\end{split}

(I.42)

In the next step we use that the functions
\textbf{h}_{n,i}(t,\hat{\bm{\beta}}_{n})
,
i=1,\ldots,n
, are
\mathcal{F}_{2}(0)
-measurable. Additionally, we apply that the values of the multiplier process
G_{i}(t)
,
t\in\mathcal{T}^{\Delta}_{n,i}
,
are independent of the
\sigma
-algebra
\mathcal{F}_{2}(0)
.
Combining these assumptions with (
I.42
), we get

\displaystyle\begin{split}&{}\mathbb{E}_{0}((D^{*,j}_{n,h}(t))^{2})\\
&{}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{l=1}^{n}\sum_{r:T_{i,r}\leq t}\sum_{v:T_{i,v}\leq t}{h}^{j}_{n,i}(T_{i,r},\hat{\bm{\beta}}_{n}){h}^{j}_{n,l}(T_{l,v},\hat{\bm{\beta}}_{n})\mathbb{E}(G_{i}(T_{i,r})G_{l}(T_{l,v})).\end{split}

(I.43)

By construction of the multiplier processes we have for
i\neq l
or
\{i=l,r\neq v\}

\mathbb{E}(G_{i}(T_{i,k})G_{l}(T_{l,v}))=\mathbb{E}(G_{i}(T_{i,k}))\mathbb{E}(G_{l}(T_{l,v}))=0,

and for
\{i=l,r=v\}

\mathbb{E}(G_{i}(T_{i,r})G_{l}(T_{l,v}))=\mathbb{E}(G_{i}(T_{i,r})^{2})=1.

Thus, (
I.43
) simplifies to
\mathbb{E}_{0}((D^{*,j}_{n,h}(t))^{2}){}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{r:T_{i,r}\leq t}{h}^{j}_{n,i}(T_{i,r},\hat{\bm{\beta}}_{n})^{2}
. Finally, it holds that

\sup_{t\in\mathcal{T}}\mathbb{E}_{0}(D^{*,j}_{n,h}(t)^{2})\leq\sup_{t\in\mathcal{T},i\in\{1,\ldots,n\}}{h}^{j}_{n,i}(t,\hat{\bm{\beta}}_{n})^{2}\cdot\max_{i\in\{1,\ldots,n\}}N_{i}(\tau)<\infty,

since
\textbf{h}_{n,i}(t,\hat{\bm{\beta}}_{n})
is a known function and hence, all components
{h}^{j}_{n,i}(t,\hat{\bm{\beta}}_{n})
,
j=1,\ldots,p+b
, are bounded on
\mathcal{T}
.
Moreover, the observed number of events within the time frame
\mathcal{T}=[0,\tau]
,
N_{i}(\tau)
, is finite for all individuals
i=1,\ldots,n
. In conclusion,
{D}^{*,j}_{n,h}(t)
,
t\in\mathcal{T}
, is square integrable for all
j=1,\ldots,p+b
, given the initial
\sigma
-algebra
\mathcal{F}_{2}(0)
.

Next, we consider the martingale property for the stochastic process
{D}^{*,j}_{n,h}(t)
,
t\in\mathcal{T}
.
Due to the linearity of the conditional expectation, is suffices to verify the martingale property for the summands
{D}^{*,j}_{n,h,i}(t)
of the scaled sum
{D}^{*,j}_{n,h}(t)
,
i=1,\ldots,n
. For this, we recall that the function
\textbf{h}_{n,i}(t,\hat{\bm{\beta}}_{n})
and the counting process
N_{i}(t)
are
\mathcal{F}_{2}(0)\subset\mathcal{F}_{2}(t)
-measurable for
t\in\mathcal{T}
, respectively,
i=1,\ldots,n
. Furthermore, for a jump at
u\leq s
, the multiplier process
G_{i}(u)
is
\mathcal{F}_{2}(s)
-measurable, and,
if
u
is greater than or equal to the earliest jump time point, say
T_{i}(s^{+})
, of process
i
in
(s,\tau]
,
the values of
G_{i}(u)
and the filtration
\mathcal{F}_{2}(s)
are independent,
i=1,\ldots,n
. Moreover, we use that the multiplier process
G_{i}(t)
,
t\in\mathcal{T}
, has mean zero. This yields for any
t>s
,

\displaystyle{}\mathbb{E}[{D}^{*,j}_{n,h,i}(t)|\mathcal{F}_{2}(s)]

\displaystyle{}=\mathbb{E}\Big[\int_{0}^{t}{h}^{j}_{n,i}(u,\hat{\bm{\beta}}_{n})G_{i}(u)\,dN_{i}(u)|\mathcal{F}_{2}(s)\Big]

\displaystyle{}=\mathbb{E}\Big[\int_{0}^{s}{h}^{j}_{n,i}(u,\hat{\bm{\beta}}_{n})G_{i}(u)\,dN_{i}(u)+\int_{s}^{t}{h}^{j}_{n,i}(u,\hat{\bm{\beta}}_{n})G_{i}(u)\,dN_{i}(u)\Big|\mathcal{F}_{2}(s)\Big]\,

\displaystyle{}={D}^{*,j}_{n,h,i}(s)+\int_{s}^{t}{h}^{j}_{n,i}(u,\hat{\bm{\beta}}_{n})\,\mathbb{E}(G_{i}(u)|\mathcal{F}_{2}(s))\,dN_{i}(u)

\displaystyle{}={D}^{*,j}_{n,h,i}(s)+\int_{T_{i}(s^{+})}^{t}{h}^{j}_{n,i}(u,\hat{\bm{\beta}}_{n})\,\mathbb{E}(G_{i}(u))\,dN_{i}(u)

\displaystyle{}={D}^{*,j}_{n,h,i}(s).

Thus, we have shown that all elements
{D}^{*,j}_{n,h}
of
\textbf{D}^{*}_{n,h}
,
j=1,\ldots,p+b
, fulfill the martingale property. In conclusion, the stochastic process
\textbf{D}^{*}_{n,h}
is a
(p+b)
-dimensional vector of square integrable martingales with respect to
\mathcal{F}_{2}(t)
,
t\in\mathcal{T}
. With this the first part of
I.3.2
has been proven.

In the second part of this proof we derive the predictable covariation process
\langle\textbf{D}^{*}_{n,h}\rangle
and the optional covariation process
[\textbf{D}^{*}_{n,h}]
of
\textbf{D}^{*}_{n,h}
. First, we consider the predictable covariation
process
\langle\textbf{D}^{*}_{n,h}\rangle(t)
:

\displaystyle\begin{split}&{}\langle\textbf{D}^{*}_{n,h}\rangle=\frac{1}{{n}}\Big\langle\sum_{i=1}^{n}({D}^{*,1}_{n,h,i},\ldots,{D}^{*,p+b}_{n,h,i})\Big\rangle\\
&{}=\frac{1}{{n}}\Big(\Big\langle\sum_{i=1}^{n}{D}^{*,j}_{n,h,i},\sum_{i=1}^{n}{D}^{*,r}_{n,h,i}\Big\rangle\Big)_{j,r=1}^{p+b}\\
&{}=\frac{1}{{n}}\Big(\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{l=1}^{n}\langle{D}^{*,j}_{n,h,i},{D}^{*,r}_{n,h,l}\rangle\Big)_{j,r=1}^{p+b}\\
&{}=\frac{1}{{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{l=i}\big(\langle{D}^{*,j}_{n,h,i},{D}^{*,r}_{n,h,l}\rangle\big)_{j,r=1}^{p+b}\quad+\quad\frac{1}{{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{l\neq i}\big(\langle{D}^{*,j}_{n,h,i},{D}^{*,r}_{n,h,l}\rangle\big)_{j,r=1}^{p+b},\end{split}

(I.44)

where in the second step of (
I.44
) we used that the predictable covariation process of a vector valued martingale is the matrix of the predictable covariation processes of its components.
In the following we consider the predictable covariation processes
\langle{D}^{*,j}_{n,h,i},{D}^{*,r}_{n,h,l}\rangle
for
i=l
and
i\neq l
separately. Recall that the functions
\textbf{h}_{n,i}(t,\hat{\bm{\beta}}_{n})
and the counting processes
N_{i}
are
\mathcal{F}_{2}(0)\subset\mathcal{F}_{2}(t)
-measurable, respectively, and that the values of the multiplier processes
G_{i}(t)
,
t\in\mathcal{T}
, are independent of the
\sigma
-algebra
\mathcal{F}_{2}(t-)
,
i=1,\ldots,n
.
We then get for
i=l
,

\displaystyle\begin{split}&{}\langle D^{*,j}_{n,h,i},D^{*,r}_{n,h,i}\rangle(t)\\
&{}=\int_{0}^{t}\text{Cov}\big(dD^{*,j}_{n,h,i}(u),dD^{*,r}_{n,h,i}(u)|\mathcal{F}_{2}(u-)\big)\\
&{}=\int_{0}^{t}\text{Cov}\big(h^{j}_{n,i}(u,\hat{\bm{\beta}}_{n})\,G_{i}(u)\,dN_{i}(u),h^{r}_{n,i}(u,\hat{\bm{\beta}}_{n})\,G_{i}(u)\,dN_{i}(u)|\mathcal{F}_{2}(u-)\big)\\
&{}=\int_{0}^{t}h^{j}_{n,i}(u,\hat{\bm{\beta}}_{n})h^{r}_{n,i}(u,\hat{\bm{\beta}}_{n})\text{Var}(G_{i}(u))dN_{i}(u)\\
&{}=\int_{0}^{t}h^{j}_{n,i}(u,\hat{\bm{\beta}}_{n})h^{r}_{n,i}(u,\hat{\bm{\beta}}_{n})dN_{i}(u),\end{split}

(I.45)

where for the last equation above we have used that the multiplier processes
G_{i}(t)
,
t\in\mathcal{T}
, have unit variance,
i=1,\ldots,n
.

For
i\neq l
it holds that

\displaystyle\begin{split}d\langle{D}^{*,j}_{n,h,i},{D}^{*,r}_{n,h,l}\rangle(t)&{}=\text{Cov}\big(dD^{*,j}_{n,h,i}(u),dD^{*,r}_{n,h,l}(u)|\mathcal{F}_{2}(u-)\big)\\
&{}=\text{Cov}\big(h_{n,i}^{j}(u,\hat{\bm{\beta}}_{n})G_{i}(u)dN_{i}(u),h_{n,l}^{r}(u,\hat{\bm{\beta}}_{n})G_{l}(u)dN_{l}(u)|\mathcal{F}_{2}(u-)\big)\\
&{}=h_{n,i}^{j}(u,\hat{\bm{\beta}}_{n})h_{n,l}^{r}(u,\hat{\bm{\beta}}_{n})\text{Cov}\big(G_{i}(u),G_{l}(u)\big)dN_{i}(u)dN_{l}(u)\\
&{}=0,\end{split}

(I.46)

where in the last step we have applied that the multiplier processes
G_{1}(t),\ldots,G_{n}(t)
,
t\in\mathcal{T}
, are pairwise independent and no two processes jump simultaneously. Hence,
\langle{D}^{*,j}_{n,h,i},{D}^{*,r}_{n,h,l}\rangle(t)=0
for
i\neq l
. Combining (
I.44
), (
I.45
), and (
I.46
), we can state the final form of the predictable covariation process
\langle\textbf{D}^{*}_{n,h}\rangle
of
\textbf{D}^{*}_{n,h}
at
t\in\mathcal{T}
in matrix notation

\displaystyle\langle\textbf{D}^{*}_{n,h}\rangle(t)

\displaystyle{}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\int_{0}^{t}\big(h^{j}_{n,i}(u,\hat{\bm{\beta}}_{n})h^{r}_{n,i}(u,\hat{\bm{\beta}}_{n})\big)_{j,r=1}^{p+b}dN_{i}(u)=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\int_{0}^{t}\textbf{h}_{n,i}(u,\hat{\bm{\beta}}_{n})^{\otimes 2}dN_{i}(u),

which proves the second part of
I.3.2
.

For the optional covariation process
[\textbf{D}^{*}_{n,h}]
of
\textbf{D}^{*}_{n,h}
we can write analogously to (
I.44
)

\displaystyle\begin{split}[\textbf{D}^{*}_{n,h}](t)&{}=\frac{1}{{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{l=i}\big([{D}^{*,j}_{n,h,i},{D}^{*,r}_{n,h,l}](t)\big)_{j,r=1}^{p+b}+\frac{1}{{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{l\neq i}\big([{D}^{*,j}_{n,h,i},{D}^{*,r}_{n,h,l}](t)\big)_{j,r=1}^{p+b}.\end{split}

(I.47)

Again, we consider the optional covariation process
[{D}^{*,j}_{n,h,i},{D}^{*,r}_{n,h,l}]
for
i=l
and
i\neq l

separately. For
i=l
we get

\displaystyle\begin{split}[D^{*,j}_{n,h,i},D^{*,r}_{n,h,i}](t)&{}=\sum_{u\leq t}\Delta D^{*,j}_{n,h,i}(u)\Delta D^{*,r}_{n,h,i}(u)\\
&{}=\sum_{u\leq t}h^{j}_{n,i}(u,\hat{\bm{\beta}}_{n})\,G_{i}(u)\,\Delta N_{i}(u)h^{r}_{n,i}(u,\hat{\bm{\beta}}_{n})\,G_{i}(u)\,\Delta N_{i}(u)\\
&{}=\int_{0}^{t}h^{j}_{n,i}(u,\hat{\bm{\beta}}_{n})h^{r}_{n,i}(u,\hat{\bm{\beta}}_{n})\,G^{2}_{i}(u)\,dN_{i}(u).\end{split}

(I.48)

For
i\neq l
it holds that

\displaystyle\begin{split}[D^{*,j}_{n,h,i},D^{*,r}_{n,h,l}](t)&{}=\sum_{u\leq t}\Delta D^{*,j}_{n,h,i}(u)\Delta D^{*,r}_{n,h,l}(u)\\
&{}=\sum_{u\leq t}h^{j}_{n,i}(u,\hat{\bm{\beta}}_{n})\,G_{i}(u)\,\Delta N_{i}(u)h^{r}_{n,l}(u,\hat{\bm{\beta}}_{n})\,G_{l}(u)\,\Delta N_{l}(u)\\
&{}=0,\end{split}

(I.49)

where in the last step of the equation above we have used that no two counting processes jump at the same time. Combining (
I.47
), (
I.48
), and (
I.49
), we find for the optional covariation process
[\textbf{D}^{*}_{n,h}]
of
\textbf{D}^{*}_{n,h}
at
t\in\mathcal{T}
in matrix notation:

\displaystyle[\textbf{D}^{*}_{n,h}](t)

\displaystyle{}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\int_{0}^{t}\big(h^{j}_{n,i}(u,\hat{\bm{\beta}}_{n})h^{r}_{n,i}(u,\hat{\bm{\beta}}_{n})\big)_{j,r=1}^{p+b}\,G_{i}(u)^{2}dN_{i}(u)

\displaystyle{}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\int_{0}^{t}\textbf{h}_{n,i}(u,\hat{\bm{\beta}}_{n})^{\otimes 2}\,G_{i}(u)^{2}dN_{i}(u),

which proves the third part of
I.3.2
and the proof of the lemma is complete.

\blacksquare

Proof of
I.3.5
.

According to
I.3.2
,
\textbf{D}_{n,h}^{*}
is a vector of square integrable martingales and its predictable covariation process takes the form

\displaystyle\langle\textbf{D}_{n,h}^{*}\rangle(t)

\displaystyle{}=\langle\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\int_{0}^{\cdot}\textbf{h}_{n,i}(u,\hat{\bm{\beta}}_{n})G_{i}(u)\,dN_{i}(u)\rangle(t)

\displaystyle{}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\int_{0}^{t}\textbf{h}_{n,i}(u,\hat{\bm{\beta}}_{n})^{\otimes 2}\,dN_{i}(u)

\displaystyle{}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\int_{0}^{t}\textbf{h}_{n,i}(u,\hat{\bm{\beta}}_{n})^{\otimes 2}\,(dM_{i}(u)+d\Lambda_{i}(u,\bm{\beta}_{0})),

where in the third step we have used the Doob-Meyer decomposition with
M_{i}
a square integrable martingale with respect to
\mathcal{F}_{1}
and
\Lambda_{i}(\cdot,\bm{\beta}_{0})
its compensator. Note the similarity of the integral with respect to
\Lambda_{i}(t,\bm{\beta}_{0})
to that of
\langle\textbf{D}_{n,h}\rangle(t)
in (
I.31
), the only difference being that the integrand is evaluated at
\hat{\bm{\beta}_{n}}
instead of at
\bm{\beta}_{0}
.
We make use of the result about
\langle\textbf{D}_{n,h}\rangle(t)
and consider

\displaystyle\begin{split}&{}\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\int_{0}^{t}\textbf{h}_{n,i}(u,\hat{\bm{\beta}}_{n})^{\otimes 2}\,d\Lambda_{i}(u,\bm{\beta}_{0})-\langle\textbf{D}_{n,h}\rangle(t)+\langle\textbf{D}_{n,h}\rangle(t)\\
&{}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\int_{0}^{t}[\textbf{h}_{n,i}(u,\hat{\bm{\beta}}_{n})^{\otimes 2}-\textbf{h}_{n,i}(u,{\bm{\beta}_{0}})^{\otimes 2}]\,d\Lambda_{i}(u,\bm{\beta}_{0})+\langle\textbf{D}_{n,h}\rangle(t),\end{split}

(I.50)

where the first term on the right-hand side can be bounded from above in the following way.

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\int_{0}^{t}[\textbf{h}_{n,i}(u,\hat{\bm{\beta}}_{n})^{\otimes 2}-\textbf{h}_{n,i}(u,{\bm{\beta}_{0}})^{\otimes 2}]\,d\Lambda_{i}(u,\bm{\beta}_{0})

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{i\in\{1,\ldots,n\},t\in\mathcal{T}}\lVert\textbf{h}_{n,i}(u,\hat{\bm{\beta}}_{n})^{\otimes 2}-\tilde{\textbf{h}}_{i}(t,\bm{\beta}_{0})^{\otimes 2}+\tilde{\textbf{h}}_{i}(t,\bm{\beta}_{0})^{\otimes 2}-\textbf{h}_{n,i}(u,{\bm{\beta}_{0}})^{\otimes 2}\rVert_{\infty}\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\Lambda_{i}(t,\bm{\beta}_{0})

\displaystyle\leq\Big(\sup_{i\in\{1,\dots,n\},t\in\mathcal{T}}\lVert(\textbf{h}_{n,i}(t,\hat{\bm{\beta}}_{n})-\tilde{\textbf{h}}_{i}(t,\bm{\beta}_{0}))\textbf{h}_{n,i}(t,\hat{\bm{\beta}}_{n})^{\top}\rVert_{\infty}

\displaystyle\qquad+\sup_{i\in\{1,\dots,n\},t\in\mathcal{T}}\lVert\tilde{\textbf{h}}_{i}(t,\bm{\beta}_{0})(\textbf{h}_{n,i}(t,\hat{\bm{\beta}}_{n})-\tilde{\textbf{h}}_{i}(t,\bm{\beta}_{0}))^{\top}\rVert_{\infty}

\displaystyle\qquad+\sup_{i\in\{1,\dots,n\},t\in\mathcal{T}}\lVert(\textbf{h}_{n,i}(t,{\bm{\beta}}_{0})-\tilde{\textbf{h}}_{i}(t,\bm{\beta}_{0}))\textbf{h}_{n,i}(t,{\bm{\beta}}_{0})^{\top}\rVert_{\infty}

\displaystyle\qquad+\sup_{i\in\{1,\dots,n\},t\in\mathcal{T}}\lVert\tilde{\textbf{h}}_{i}(t,\bm{\beta}_{0})(\textbf{h}_{n,i}(t,{\bm{\beta}}_{0})-\tilde{\textbf{h}}_{i}(t,\bm{\beta}_{0}))^{\top}\rVert_{\infty}\Big)\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\Lambda_{i}(t,\bm{\beta}_{0}).

All four terms in brackets converge to zero in probability, as
n\rightarrow\infty
, according to
I.2.1

(i)
,
(ii)
, and the fact that
\textbf{h}_{n,i}(t,{\bm{\beta}}_{0})
and
\textbf{h}_{n,i}(t,{\hat{\bm{\beta}}}_{n})
are (locally) bounded. In the following we make use of results of the proof of
I.2.2
. For this we note that convergence in probability is equivalent to convergence in conditional probability, cf. Fact 1 of the supplement of
Dobler et al. 2019
. As stated in the proof of
I.2.2
,
\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\Lambda_{i}(t,\bm{\beta}_{0})=O_{p}(1)
, according to
I.2.1

(iii)
, the integrability of
\Lambda_{i}(t,\bm{\beta}_{0})
and the law of large numbers. Hence, the first term on the right-hand side of (
I.50
) converges to zero in probability, as
n\rightarrow\infty
. Additionally, according to
I.2.1

(ii)
,
(iii)
, the integrability of
\Lambda_{i}(t,\bm{\beta}_{0})
and the law of large numbers, we have shown in the proof of
I.2.2
that

\displaystyle\langle\textbf{D}_{n,h}\rangle(t)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathbb{P}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\int_{0}^{t}\mathbb{E}\Big(\tilde{\textbf{h}}_{1}(u,\bm{\beta}_{0})^{\otimes 2}\lambda_{1}(u,\bm{\beta}_{0})\Big)du=\textbf{V}_{\tilde{h}}(t),\text{ for all }t\in\mathcal{T},\text{ as }n\rightarrow\infty;

cf.
I.2.1

(iii)
. In particular,
\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\int_{0}^{t}\textbf{h}_{n,i}(u,\hat{\bm{\beta}}_{n})^{\otimes 2}\,d\Lambda_{i}(u,\bm{\beta}_{0})
and
\langle\textbf{D}_{n,h}\rangle(t)
are asymptotically equivalent.

Next, we consider the integral with respect to the local square integrable martingale
M_{i}
,
i=1,\ldots,n
. As, conditionally on
\mathcal{F}_{2}(0)
, the integrands
\textbf{h}_{n,i}(\cdot,\hat{\bm{\beta}})^{\otimes 2}
,
i=1,\ldots,n
, are known and, hence, predictable with respect to
\mathcal{F}_{2}

and locally bounded, the corresponding integral
\textbf{W}_{n}(t)=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\int_{0}^{t}\textbf{h}_{n,i}(u,\hat{\bm{\beta}})^{\otimes 2}\,dM_{i}(u)
is a local square integrable martingale (Proposition II.4.1,
Andersen et al. 1993
). Hence, we apply Lenglart’s inequality in order to show that
\textbf{W}_{n}(t)
converges to zero in probability for all
t\in\mathcal{T}
, as
n\rightarrow\infty
.
For this purpose, we consider its predictable covariation process

\displaystyle\begin{split}\langle\text{vec}(\textbf{W}_{n})\rangle(\tau)&{}=\langle\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\int_{0}^{\cdot}\text{vec}(\textbf{h}_{n,i}(u,\hat{\bm{\beta}})^{\otimes 2})\,dM_{i}(u)\rangle(\tau)\\
&{}=\frac{1}{n^{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\int_{0}^{\tau}\text{vec}(\textbf{h}_{n,i}(u,\hat{\bm{\beta}})^{\otimes 2})^{\otimes 2}\,d\Lambda_{i}(u,\bm{\beta}_{0}),\\
&{}=\frac{1}{n^{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\int_{0}^{\tau}[\text{vec}(\textbf{h}_{n,i}(u,\hat{\bm{\beta}})^{\otimes 2})^{\otimes 2}-\text{vec}(\tilde{\textbf{h}}_{i}(u,{\bm{\beta}_{0}})^{\otimes 2})^{\otimes 2}]\,d\Lambda_{i}(u,\bm{\beta}_{0})\\
&{}\quad+\frac{1}{n^{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\int_{0}^{\tau}\text{vec}(\tilde{\textbf{h}}_{i}(u,{\bm{\beta}_{0}})^{\otimes 2})^{\otimes 2}\,d\Lambda_{i}(u,\bm{\beta}_{0}),\end{split}

(I.51)

where in the second equality it has been used that the martingales
M_{1}(t),\dots,M_{n}(t)
are independent. We wish to show that the first term on the right-hand side of the third step
converges to zero in probability, as
n\rightarrow\infty
. For this, it suffices to consider the largest component

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n^{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\int_{0}^{\tau}\lVert\text{vec}(\textbf{h}_{n,i}(u,\hat{\bm{\beta}}_{n})^{\otimes 2})^{\otimes 2}-\text{vec}(\tilde{\textbf{h}}_{i}(u,{\bm{\beta}_{0}})^{\otimes 2})^{\otimes 2}\rVert_{\infty}\,d\Lambda_{i}(u,\bm{\beta}_{0})

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{i\in\{1\ldots,n\},t\in\mathcal{T}}\lVert\text{vec}(\textbf{h}_{n,i}(t,\hat{\bm{\beta}}_{n})^{\otimes 2})^{\otimes 2}-\text{vec}(\tilde{\textbf{h}}_{i}(t,{\bm{\beta}_{0}})^{\otimes 2})^{\otimes 2}\rVert_{\infty}\frac{1}{n^{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\Lambda_{i}(\tau,\bm{\beta}_{0}).

It holds that

\displaystyle{}\lVert\text{vec}(\textbf{h}_{n,i}(t,\hat{\bm{\beta}}_{n})^{\otimes 2})^{\otimes 2}-\text{vec}(\tilde{\textbf{h}}_{i}(t,{\bm{\beta}_{0}})^{\otimes 2})^{\otimes 2}\rVert_{\infty}

\displaystyle{}\leq\quad\rVert\textbf{h}_{n,i}(t,\hat{\bm{\beta}}_{n})\lVert_{\infty}^{2}\Big[\lVert\textbf{h}_{n,i}(t,\hat{\bm{\beta}}_{n})-\tilde{\textbf{h}}_{i}(t,{\bm{\beta}_{0}})\rVert_{\infty}\lVert\textbf{h}_{n,i}(t,\hat{\bm{\beta}}_{n})\rVert_{\infty}

\displaystyle{}\quad+\lVert\tilde{\textbf{h}}_{i}(t,{\bm{\beta}_{0}})\rVert_{\infty}\lVert\textbf{h}_{n,i}(t,\hat{\bm{\beta}}_{n})-\tilde{\textbf{h}}_{i}(t,{\bm{\beta}_{0}})\rVert_{\infty}\Big]

\displaystyle{}\quad+\lVert\tilde{\textbf{h}}_{i}(t,{\bm{\beta}_{0}})\rVert_{\infty}^{2}\Big[\lVert\textbf{h}_{n,i}(t,\hat{\bm{\beta}}_{n})-\tilde{\textbf{h}}_{i}(t,{\bm{\beta}_{0}})\rVert_{\infty}\lVert\textbf{h}_{n,i}(t,\hat{\bm{\beta}}_{n})\rVert_{\infty}

\displaystyle{}\quad+\lVert\tilde{\textbf{h}}_{i}(t,{\bm{\beta}_{0}})\rVert_{\infty}\lVert\textbf{h}_{n,i}(t,\hat{\bm{\beta}}_{n})-\tilde{\textbf{h}}_{i}(t,{\bm{\beta}_{0}})\rVert_{\infty}\Big],

where we used the triangle inequality and applied
\textbf{a}^{\otimes 2}-\textbf{b}^{\otimes 2}=(\textbf{a}-\textbf{b})\textbf{a}^{\top}+\textbf{b}(\textbf{a}-\textbf{b})^{\top}
for two vectors
\textbf{a},\textbf{b}
twice, i.e.,

\displaystyle\begin{split}\text{vec}[\textbf{a}^{\otimes 2}]^{\otimes 2}-\text{vec}[\textbf{b}^{\otimes 2}]^{\otimes 2}&=\text{vec}[(\textbf{a}-\textbf{b})\textbf{a}^{\top}+\textbf{b}(\textbf{a}-\textbf{b})^{\top}]\text{vec}[\textbf{a}\textbf{a}^{\top}]^{\top}\\
&\quad+\text{vec}[\textbf{b}\textbf{b}^{\top}]\text{vec}[(\textbf{a}-\textbf{b})\textbf{a}^{\top}+\textbf{b}(\textbf{a}-\textbf{b})^{\top}]^{\top}.\end{split}

(I.52)

Hence, according to
I.2.1

(i)
,
(ii)
, and since
\textbf{h}_{n,i}(t,\hat{\bm{\beta}}_{n})
is locally bounded for
i=1,\ldots,n
, it follows that
\sup_{i\in\{1\ldots,n\},t\in\mathcal{T}}\lVert\text{vec}(\textbf{h}_{n,i}(u,\hat{\bm{\beta}}_{n})^{\otimes 2})^{\otimes 2}-\text{vec}(\tilde{\textbf{h}}_{i}(u,{\bm{\beta}_{0}})^{\otimes 2})^{\otimes 2}\rVert_{\infty}=o_{p}(1)
. As explained before, we have
\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\Lambda_{i}(\tau,\bm{\beta}_{0})=O_{p}(1)
. In conclusion, the first term on the right-hand side of the third step of (
I.51
) converges to zero in probability, as
n\rightarrow\infty
.

We futher need to show that the corresponding second term vanishes asymptotically. For this we consider the largest component of
{\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\Big(\int_{0}^{\tau}\text{vec}(\tilde{\textbf{h}}_{1}(u,\bm{\beta}_{0})^{\otimes 2})^{\otimes 2}\,d\Lambda_{1}(u,\bm{\beta}_{0})\Big)}
, for which it holds that

\mathbb{E}(\int_{0}^{\tau}\lVert\tilde{\textbf{h}}_{1}(u,\bm{\beta}_{0})\rVert_{\infty}^{4}\,d\Lambda_{1}(u,\bm{\beta}_{0}))=\mathbb{E}(\sup_{t\in\mathcal{T}}\lVert\tilde{\textbf{h}}_{1}(t,\bm{\beta}_{0})\rVert_{\infty}^{4}\Lambda_{1}(\tau,\bm{\beta}_{0}))<\infty,

due to
I.2.1

(ii)
and the integrability of
\Lambda_{i}(\tau,\bm{\beta}_{0})
. Combining this with

I.2.1

(iii)
and the law of large numbers yields

\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\int_{0}^{\tau}\text{vec}(\tilde{\textbf{h}}_{i}(u,\bm{\beta}_{0})^{\otimes 2})^{\otimes 2}\,d\Lambda_{i}(u,\bm{\beta}_{0})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathbb{P}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathbb{E}\Big(\int_{0}^{\tau}\text{vec}(\tilde{\textbf{h}}_{1}(u,\bm{\beta}_{0})^{\otimes 2})^{\otimes 2}\,d\Lambda_{1}(u,\bm{\beta}_{0})\Big),

as
n\rightarrow\infty
.
Finally, for the second term on the right-hand side of the third step of (
I.51
) we have

{\displaystyle\frac{1}{n^{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\int_{0}^{\tau}\text{vec}(\tilde{\textbf{k}}_{i}(t,{\bm{\beta}_{0}})^{\otimes 2})^{\otimes 2}\,d\Lambda_{i}(u,\bm{\beta}_{0})=o(1)\cdot O_{p}(1)}
.
Thus,
\textbf{W}_{n}(t)
converges to zero in probability for all
t\in\mathcal{T}
, as
n\rightarrow\infty
, according to Lenglart’s inequality.
In conclusion, the predictable covariation process
\langle\textbf{D}_{n,h}^{*}\rangle(t)
of
\textbf{D}_{n,h}^{*}
at
t
converges to the matrix-valued function
{\displaystyle\textbf{V}_{\tilde{h}}(t)=\int_{0}^{t}\mathbb{E}(\tilde{\textbf{h}}_{1}(u,\bm{\beta}_{0})^{\otimes 2}\,\lambda_{1}(u,\bm{\beta}_{0}))du}
in probability, as
n\rightarrow\infty
, for all
t\in\mathcal{T}
(cf.
I.2.1

(iii)
).
This completes the proof of
I.3.5
.

\blacksquare

Proof of
I.3.6
.

We use the modified version of Rebolledo’s central limit theorem as stated in
I.3.4
to prove the weak convergence of
\textbf{D}_{n,h}^{*}
to the zero-mean Gaussian martingale
\textbf{D}_{\tilde{h}}
. For this purpose, we first consider the term
\sigma^{\epsilon}[\bm{\lambda}^{\top}\textbf{D}_{n,h}^{*}](\tau)
for some
\bm{\lambda}\in{S}^{p+b-1},
where
{S}^{p+b-1}
denotes the unit
(p+b-1)
-sphere. It can be seen that

\displaystyle\sigma^{\epsilon}[\bm{\lambda}^{\top}\textbf{D}_{n,h}^{*}](\tau)=\sum_{u\leq\tau}\lvert\Delta\bm{\lambda}^{\top}\textbf{D}_{n,h}^{*}(u)\rvert^{2}\mathbbm{1}\{\lvert\Delta\bm{\lambda}^{\top}\textbf{D}_{n,h}^{*}(u)\rvert>\epsilon\}

\displaystyle=\sum_{u\leq\tau}\lvert\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\bm{\lambda}^{\top}\textbf{h}_{n,i}(u,\hat{\bm{\beta}}_{n})G_{i}(u)\,\Delta N_{i}(u)\rvert^{2}\mathbbm{1}\{\lvert\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\bm{\lambda}^{\top}\textbf{h}_{n,i}(u,\hat{\bm{\beta}}_{n})G_{i}(u)\,\Delta N_{i}(u)\rvert>\epsilon\}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{n}\sum_{u\leq\tau}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\lvert\bm{\lambda}^{\top}\textbf{h}_{n,i}(u,\hat{\bm{\beta}}_{n})G_{i}(u)\,\Delta N_{i}(u)\rvert^{2}\mathbbm{1}\{\lvert\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\bm{\lambda}^{\top}\textbf{h}_{n,i}(u,\hat{\bm{\beta}}_{n})G_{i}(u)\,\Delta N_{i}(u)\rvert>\epsilon\}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j:T_{i,j}\in\mathcal{T}_{n,i}^{\Delta}}(\bm{\lambda}^{\top}\textbf{h}_{n,i}(T_{i,j},\hat{\bm{\beta}}_{n}))^{2}G_{i}^{2}(T_{i,j})\mathbbm{1}\{\lvert\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\bm{\lambda}^{\top}\textbf{h}_{n,i}(T_{i,j},\hat{\bm{\beta}}_{n})G_{i}(T_{i,j})\rvert>\epsilon\},

where in the third step of the derivation above it has been used that no two counting processes jump at the same time, i.e.,
\Delta N_{i}(t)\Delta N_{j}(t)=0
, for
i\neq j
.
From this it follows that

\displaystyle{}\mathbb{E}_{0}(\sigma^{\epsilon}[\bm{\lambda}^{\top}\textbf{D}_{n,h}^{*}](\tau))

\displaystyle{}\leq\mathbb{E}_{0}(\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j:T_{i,j}\in\mathcal{T}_{n,i}^{\Delta}}(\bm{\lambda}^{\top}\textbf{h}_{n,i}(T_{i,j},\hat{\bm{\beta}}_{n}))^{2}G_{i}^{2}(T_{i,j})\mathbbm{1}\{\lvert\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\bm{\lambda}^{\top}\textbf{h}_{n,i}(T_{i,j},\hat{\bm{\beta}}_{n})G_{i}(T_{i,j})\rvert>\epsilon\})

\displaystyle{}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j:T_{i,j}\in\mathcal{T}_{n,i}^{\Delta}}(\bm{\lambda}^{\top}\textbf{h}_{n,i}(T_{i,j},\hat{\bm{\beta}}_{n}))^{2}\mathbb{E}_{0}(G_{i}^{2}(T_{i,j})\mathbbm{1}\{\lvert\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\bm{\lambda}^{\top}\textbf{h}_{n,i}(T_{i,j},\hat{\bm{\beta}}_{n})G_{i}(T_{i,j})\rvert>\epsilon\})

\displaystyle{}\leq\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j:T_{i,j}\in\mathcal{T}_{n,i}^{\Delta}}(\bm{\lambda}^{\top}\textbf{h}_{n,i}(T_{i,j},\hat{\bm{\beta}}_{n}))^{2}\big(\mathbb{E}(G_{1,1}^{4})\mathbb{P}_{0}(\lvert\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\bm{\lambda}^{\top}\textbf{h}_{n,i}(T_{i,j},\hat{\bm{\beta}}_{n})G_{1,1}\rvert>\epsilon)\big)^{1/2}

\displaystyle{}\leq\sup_{t\in\mathcal{T},i\in\{1,\ldots,n\}}(\bm{\lambda}^{\top}\textbf{h}_{n,i}(t,\hat{\bm{\beta}}_{n}))^{2}(\mathbb{E}(G_{1,1}^{4}))^{1/2}[\mathbb{P}_{0}(\sup_{t\in\mathcal{T},i\in\{1,\ldots,n\}}\lvert\bm{\lambda}^{\top}\textbf{h}_{n,i}(t,\hat{\bm{\beta}}_{n})\rvert\lvert G_{1,1}\rvert>\epsilon\sqrt{n})]^{1/2}

\displaystyle{}\quad\cdot\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}N_{i}(\tau),

where
\mathbb{E}_{0}(\cdot)
and
\mathbb{P}_{0}(\cdot)
denote the conditional expectation
\mathbb{E}(\cdot|\mathcal{F}_{2}(0))
and the conditional probability
\mathbb{P}(\cdot|\mathcal{F}_{2}(0))
, respectively, given the initial filtration
\mathcal{F}_{2}(0)
. In the first step of the equation above we have used that
\textbf{h}_{n,i}(t,\hat{\bm{\beta}})\in\mathcal{F}_{2}(0)
. In the second step, the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality has been applied. In the same step it has additionally been used that the multiplier processes
G_{i}(t)
,
t\in\mathcal{T},i=1,\ldots,n,
are i.i.d. and independent of
\mathcal{F}_{2}(0)
. As our first goal is to verify the conditional Lindeberg condition in probability, i.e.,
\mathbb{E}_{0}(\sigma^{\epsilon}[\bm{\lambda}^{\top}\textbf{D}_{n,h}^{*}](\tau))\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathbb{P}}}{{\longrightarrow}}0
,
n\rightarrow\infty
, we point out that for the terms of the last step of the equation above we have
\mathbb{E}(G_{1,1}^{4})<\infty
and
\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}N_{i}(\tau)=O_{p}(1)
. The latter holds according to the integrability of
\Lambda_{i}(\tau,\mathbf{{\beta}}_{0})
and
I.2.1

(iii)
, as explained at the beginning of the proof of
I.2.4
in combination with Fact 1 of the supplement of
Dobler et al. 2019
. Furthermore, the limiting function
\tilde{\textbf{h}}_{i}(t,\bm{\beta}_{0})
of
\textbf{h}_{n,i}(t,\hat{\bm{\beta}}_{n})
exists and is assumed to be bounded on
\mathcal{T}
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
, according to
I.2.1

(i)
and
(ii)
. Therefore,
\sup_{t\in\mathcal{T},i\in\{1,\ldots,n\}}(\bm{\lambda}^{\top}\textbf{h}_{n,i}(t,\hat{\bm{\beta}}))^{2}
is stochastically bounded:

\displaystyle\sup_{t\in\mathcal{T},i\in\{1,\ldots,n\}}(\bm{\lambda}^{\top}\textbf{h}_{n,i}(t,\hat{\bm{\beta}}_{n}))^{2}

\displaystyle\leq(p+b)\sup_{t\in\mathcal{T},i\in\{1,\ldots,n\}}\sum_{j=1}^{p+b}\lambda_{j}^{2}\textbf{h}_{n,i}^{j}(t,\hat{\bm{\beta}}_{n}))^{2}

\displaystyle\leq(p+b)^{2}\lVert\bm{\lambda}\rVert_{\infty}^{2}\sup_{t\in\mathcal{T},i\in\{1,\ldots,n\}}\lVert\textbf{h}_{n,i}(t,\hat{\bm{\beta}}_{n}))-\tilde{\textbf{h}}_{i}(t,\bm{\beta}_{0}))+\tilde{\textbf{h}}_{i}(t,\bm{\beta}_{0}))\rVert_{\infty}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq 2(p+b)^{2}\lVert\bm{\lambda}\rVert_{\infty}^{2}\big(\sup_{t\in\mathcal{T},i\in\{1,\ldots,n\}}\lVert\textbf{h}_{n,i}(t,\hat{\bm{\beta}}_{n}))-\tilde{\textbf{h}}_{i}(t,\bm{\beta}_{0}))\rVert_{\infty}^{2}+\sup_{t\in\mathcal{T},i\in\{1,\ldots,n\}}\lVert\tilde{\textbf{h}}_{i}(t,\bm{\beta}_{0}))\rVert_{\infty}^{2}\big)

\displaystyle=2(p+b)^{2}\lVert\bm{\lambda}\rVert_{\infty}^{2}\big(o_{p}(1)+\sup_{t\in\mathcal{T},i\in\{1,\ldots,n\}}\lVert\tilde{\textbf{h}}_{i}(t,\bm{\beta}_{0}))\rVert_{\infty}^{2}\big)

\displaystyle=O_{p}(1).

Hence, it is only left to show that
\mathbb{P}(\sup_{t\in\mathcal{T},i\in\{1,\ldots,n\}}\lvert\bm{\lambda}^{\top}\textbf{h}_{n,i}(t,\hat{\bm{\beta}}_{n})\rvert\lvert G_{1,1}\rvert>\epsilon\sqrt{n}|\mathcal{F}_{2}(0))=o_{p}(1)
. For this purpose, recall that
\mathbbm{1}\{\sup_{t\in\mathcal{T},i\in\{1,\ldots,n\}}\lVert\textbf{h}_{n,i}(t,\hat{\bm{\beta}}_{n})-\tilde{\textbf{h}}_{i}(t,\bm{\beta}_{0})\rVert_{\infty}<\delta\}
converges to one in probability, according to
I.2.1

(i)
.
Thus, we can proceed with the following term:

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{0}(\sup_{t\in\mathcal{T},i\in\{1,\ldots,n\}}\lvert\bm{\lambda}^{\top}\textbf{h}_{n,i}(t,\hat{\bm{\beta}}_{n})\rvert\lvert G_{1,1}\rvert>\sqrt{n}\epsilon)\mathbbm{1}\{\sup_{t\in\mathcal{T},i\in\{1,\ldots,n\}}\lVert\textbf{h}_{n,i}(t,\hat{\bm{\beta}}_{n})-\tilde{\textbf{h}}_{i}(t,\bm{\beta}_{0})\rVert_{\infty}<\delta\}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}_{0}(\sup_{t\in\mathcal{T},i\in\{1,\ldots,n\}}\lvert\bm{\lambda}^{\top}\textbf{h}_{n,i}(t,\hat{\bm{\beta}}_{n})-\bm{\lambda}^{\top}\tilde{\textbf{h}}_{i}(t,\bm{\beta}_{0})+\bm{\lambda}^{\top}\tilde{\textbf{h}}_{i}(t,\bm{\beta}_{0})\rvert\lvert G_{1,1}\rvert>\sqrt{n}\epsilon,

\displaystyle\quad\sup_{t\in\mathcal{T},i\in\{1,\ldots,n\}}\lVert\textbf{h}_{n,i}(t,\hat{\bm{\beta}}_{n})-\tilde{\textbf{h}}_{i}(t,\bm{\beta}_{0})\rVert_{\infty}<\delta)

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{P}_{0}((p+b)\lVert\bm{\lambda}\rVert_{\infty}(\delta+\sup_{t\in\mathcal{T},i\in\{1,\ldots,n\}}\lVert\tilde{\textbf{h}}_{i}(t,\bm{\beta}_{0})\rVert_{\infty})\lvert G_{1,1}\rvert>\sqrt{n}\epsilon)

\displaystyle\quad\cdot\mathbbm{1}\{\sup_{t\in\mathcal{T},i\in\{1,\ldots,n\}}\lVert\textbf{h}_{n,i}(t,\hat{\bm{\beta}}_{n})-\tilde{\textbf{h}}_{i}(t,\bm{\beta}_{0})\rVert_{\infty}<\delta\}

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{P}_{0}\big(\lvert G_{1,1}\rvert>\frac{\sqrt{n}\epsilon}{(p+b)\lVert\bm{\lambda}\rVert_{\infty}(\delta+\sup_{t\in\mathcal{T},i\in\{1,\ldots,n\}}\lVert\tilde{\textbf{h}}_{i}(t,\bm{\beta}_{0})\rVert_{\infty})}\big)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathbb{P}}}{{\longrightarrow}}0,\;n\rightarrow\infty.

Here, the convergence in probability of the conditional probability in the last step holds, because
\tilde{\textbf{h}}_{i}(t,\bm{\beta}_{0})
is bounded on
\mathcal{T}
for all
i\in\mathbb{N}
, as stated in
I.2.1

(ii)
. We can conclude that
\mathbb{P}(\sup_{t\in\mathcal{T},i\in\{1,\ldots,n\}}\lvert\bm{\lambda}^{\top}\textbf{h}_{n,i}(t,\hat{\bm{\beta}}_{n})\rvert\lvert G_{1,1}\rvert>\epsilon\sqrt{n}|\mathcal{F}_{2}(0))=o_{p}(1)
. Thus, the conditional Lindeberg condition in probability is fulfilled for
\bm{\lambda}^{\top}\textbf{D}^{*}_{n,h}(t)
with
\bm{\lambda}\in S^{p+b-1}
. As
\lVert\bm{\lambda}\rVert_{\infty}\leq 1
, we can get an upper bound independent of
\bm{\lambda}
, and thus we in fact know that the asserted Lindeberg condition holds for all
\bm{\lambda}\in S^{p+b-1}
.
We would like to point out that the probability space can more conveniently be modelled as a product space

(\Omega,\mathcal{A},\mathbb{P})=(\Omega_{1}\times\Omega_{2},\mathcal{A}_{1}\otimes\mathcal{A}_{2},\mathbb{P}_{1}\otimes\mathbb{P}_{2})=(\Omega_{1},\mathcal{A}_{1},\mathbb{P}_{1})\otimes(\Omega_{2},\mathcal{A}_{2},\mathbb{P}_{2})
.
In the following we make use of this notation to explicitly refer to the probability space
(\Omega_{1},\mathcal{A}_{1},\mathbb{P}_{1})
underlying the data sets
\{\textbf{N}(t),\textbf{Y}(t),\textbf{Z}(t),t\in\mathcal{T}\}
, and the probability space
(\Omega_{2},\mathcal{A}_{2},\mathbb{P}_{2})
underlying the sets of multiplier processes
\{G_{1}(t),\ldots,G_{n}(t)
,
t\in\mathcal{T}\}
. Additionally, we denote by
\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb{P}_{2}}}}{{\longrightarrow}}
the convergence in law w.r.t the probability measure
\mathbb{P}_{2}
. Moreover, for some stochastic quantity
\textbf{H}_{n}
, we denote
\textbf{H}_{n}
conditional on a particular data set as
\textbf{H}_{n}|\mathcal{F}_{2}(0)(\omega)
,
\omega\in\Omega_{1}
. From the conditional Lindeberg condition in probability it follows that there exists for all subsequences
n_{1}
of
n
a further subsequence
n_{2}
such that
\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}_{2}}(\sigma^{\epsilon}[\bm{\lambda}^{\top}\textbf{D}_{n_{2},h}^{*}](\tau)|\mathcal{F}_{2}(0))(\omega)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\longrightarrow}}0
,
n\rightarrow\infty
, for
\mathbb{P}_{1}
-almost all
\omega\in\Omega_{1}
and for all
\bm{\lambda}\in S^{p+b-1}
. Here,
\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}_{2}}(\cdot)
indicates that the expectation is taken with respect to
\mathbb{P}_{2}
. Hence, the (unconditional) Lindeberg condition holds along the subsequence
n_{2}
for
\mathbb{P}_{1}
-almost all data sets.

Next, we consider the predictable covariation process of
\bm{\lambda}^{\top}\textbf{D}_{n,h}^{*}
for some
\bm{\lambda}\in{S}^{p+b-1}
and get, conditionally on
\mathcal{F}_{2}(0)
,

\langle\bm{\lambda}^{\top}\textbf{D}_{n,h}^{*}\rangle(t)=\bm{\lambda}^{\top}\langle\textbf{D}_{n,h}^{*}\rangle(t)\bm{\lambda}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathbb{P}_{1}\otimes\mathbb{P}_{2}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\bm{\lambda}^{\top}\textbf{V}_{\tilde{h}}(t)\bm{\lambda},\text{ as }n\rightarrow\infty,\text{ for all }t\in\mathcal{T},

according to
I.3.5
.
Furthermore, we have

\displaystyle\bm{\lambda}^{\top}\big((\langle\textbf{D}_{n,h}^{*}\rangle-\textbf{V}_{\tilde{h}})(t)\big)\bm{\lambda}=\sum_{j=1}^{p+b}\sum_{l=1}^{p+b}\lambda_{j}(\langle\textbf{D}_{n,h}^{*}\rangle-\textbf{V}_{\tilde{h}})_{j,l}(t)\lambda_{l}

\displaystyle\leq(p+b)^{2}\lVert\bm{\lambda}\rVert_{\infty}^{2}\cdot\lVert\langle\textbf{D}_{n,h}^{*}\rangle(t)-\textbf{V}_{\tilde{h}}(t)\rVert_{\infty},

where
(\langle\textbf{D}_{n,h}^{*}\rangle-\textbf{V}_{\tilde{h}})_{j,l}
denotes the
(j,l)
-th entry of the corresponding matrix.
As
\lVert\bm{\lambda}\rVert_{\infty}\leq 1
and
\lVert\langle\textbf{D}_{n,h}^{*}\rangle(t)-\textbf{V}_{\tilde{h}}(t)\rVert_{\infty}=o_{p}(1)
, in view of
I.3.5
we thus obtain

\langle\bm{\lambda}^{\top}\textbf{D}_{n,h}^{*}\rangle(t)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathbb{P}_{1}\otimes\mathbb{P}_{2}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\bm{\lambda}^{\top}\textbf{V}_{\tilde{h}}(t)\bm{\lambda},\text{ as }n\rightarrow\infty,\text{ for all }t\in\mathcal{T},\text{ and all }\bm{\lambda}\in{S}^{p+b-1}.

Hence, there exists for every subsequenece
n_{3}
of
n_{2}
a further subsequence
n_{4}
such that
\langle\bm{\lambda}^{\top}\textbf{D}^{*}_{n_{4},h}\rangle|\mathcal{F}_{2}(0)(\omega)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathbb{P}_{2}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\bm{\lambda}^{\top}\textbf{V}_{\tilde{h}}(t)\bm{\lambda},\text{ as }n\rightarrow\infty,
for
\mathbb{P}_{1}
-almost all
\omega\in\Omega_{1}
, all
t\in\mathcal{T}
, and all
\bm{\lambda}\in{S}^{p+b-1}
.
Clearly, it also holds that
\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}_{2}}(\sigma^{\epsilon}[\bm{\lambda}^{\top}\textbf{D}_{n_{4},h}^{*}](\tau)|\mathcal{F}_{2}(0))(\omega)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\rightarrow}}0
,
n\rightarrow\infty
, for
\mathbb{P}_{1}
-almost all
\omega\in\Omega_{1}
and all
\bm{\lambda}\in S^{p+b-1}
.
Thus, with
I.3.4
it follows that

\bm{\lambda}^{\top}\textbf{D}^{*}_{n_{4},h}|\mathcal{F}_{2}(0)(\omega)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb{P}_{2}}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\bm{\lambda}^{\top}\textbf{D}_{\tilde{h}},\text{ in }D(\mathcal{T}),\text{ as }n\rightarrow\infty,

for
\mathbb{P}_{1}
-almost all
\omega\in\Omega_{1}
and all
\bm{\lambda}\in{S}^{p+b-1}
. As the weak convergence of
\bm{\lambda}^{\top}\textbf{D}^{*}_{n_{4},h}|\mathcal{F}_{2}(0)(\omega)
holds for all
\bm{\lambda}\in{S}^{p+b-1}
, the Cramér-Wold device yields
\textbf{D}^{*}_{n_{4},h}|\mathcal{F}_{2}(0)(\omega)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb{P}_{2}}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\textbf{D}_{\tilde{h}},
in
D(\mathcal{T})^{p+b},
as
n\rightarrow\infty,
for
\mathbb{P}_{1}
-almost all
\omega\in\Omega_{1}
. Finally, we get, conditionally on
\mathcal{F}_{2}(0)
,

\textbf{D}^{*}_{n,h}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb{P}_{2}}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\textbf{D}_{\tilde{h}},\text{ in }D(\mathcal{T})^{p+b},\text{ as }n\rightarrow\infty,

in
\mathbb{P}_{1}
-probability. This completes the proof of
I.3.6
.
\blacksquare

Proof of
I.3.7
.

We relate the optional covariation process
[\textbf{D}^{*}_{n,h}](t)
and the predictable covariation process
\langle\textbf{D}^{*}_{n,h}\rangle(t)
of
\textbf{D}_{n,h}^{*}(t)
to each other by noting the obvious

[\textbf{D}^{*}_{n,h}](t)=[\textbf{D}^{*}_{n,h}](t)-\langle\textbf{D}^{*}_{n,h}\rangle(t)+\langle\textbf{D}^{*}_{n,h}\rangle(t).

Consequently, if the predictable covariation process
\langle\textbf{D}^{*}_{n,h}\rangle(t)
converges in probability to
\textbf{V}_{\tilde{h}}(t)
, as
n\rightarrow\infty
, and it holds that
[\textbf{D}^{*}_{n,h}](t)-\langle\textbf{D}^{*}_{n,h}\rangle(t)=o_{p}(1)
, then also the optional covariation
[\textbf{D}^{*}_{n,h}](t)
converges in probability to
\textbf{V}_{\tilde{h}}(t)
, as
n\rightarrow\infty
, and vice versa. Hence, for this proof we assume that
I.3.5
holds and show that the difference between the optional covariation process and the predictable covariation process of
\textbf{D}_{n,h}^{*}(t)
vanishes asymptotically.

Let us consider the vectorized version
\textbf{Q}_{n}
of the difference between the optional covariation process and the predictable covariation process of
\textbf{D}_{n,h}^{*}(t)
,

t\in\mathcal{T}
,

\displaystyle\textbf{Q}_{n}(t)

\displaystyle=\text{vec}([\textbf{D}^{*}_{n,h}](t)-\langle\textbf{D}^{*}_{n,h}\rangle(t))

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\int_{0}^{t}\text{vec}(\textbf{h}_{n,i}(u,\hat{\bm{\beta}}_{n})^{\otimes 2})(G_{i}^{2}(u)-1)dN_{i}(u).

The
\text{vec}(\textbf{h}_{n,i}(t,\hat{\bm{\beta}}_{n})^{\otimes 2})
in the integrands are known and locally bounded and predictable. Hence, according to Theorem II.3.1 of
Andersen et al. 1993
,

\textbf{Q}_{n}
is a vector of local square integrable martingales if
\int_{0}^{\cdot}(G_{i}^{2}(u)-1)dN_{i}(u)
is a finite variation local square integrable martingale for all
i=1,\ldots,n
.
This is what we show in the following three steps.

1.

The finite variation holds, because

\int_{0}^{\tau}\lvert(G_{i}^{2}(u)-1)dN_{i}(u)\rvert\leq(\sup_{t\in\mathcal{T}}G_{i}^{2}(t)+1)N_{i}(\tau),

and the term on the right-hand side is almost surely finite as
N_{i}(\tau)<\infty
, and the supremum is a maximum of almost surely finitely many random variables.

2.

It is square integrable, since

\displaystyle\sup_{t\in\mathcal{T}}\mathbb{E}_{0}\Big(\Big[\int_{0}^{t}(G_{i}^{2}(u)-1)dN_{i}(u)\Big]^{2}\Big)

\displaystyle=\sup_{t\in\mathcal{T}}\mathbb{E}_{0}\Big(\Big[\sum_{j:T_{i,j}\leq t}(G_{i,j}^{2}-1)\Big]^{2}\Big)

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{t\in\mathcal{T}}\mathbb{E}_{0}\Big(\big\lvert\{j:T_{i,j}\leq t\}\big\rvert\sum_{j:T_{i,j}\leq t}(G_{i,j}^{2}-1)^{2}\Big)

\displaystyle\leq N_{i}(\tau)\sum_{j=1}^{n_{i}}\mathbb{E}(G_{i,j}^{4}-2G_{i,j}^{2}+1)

\displaystyle\leq N_{i}(\tau)^{2}\mathbb{E}(G_{1,1}^{4})<\infty,

where
\mathbb{E}_{0}(\cdot)
denotes the conditional expectation
\mathbb{E}(\cdot|\mathcal{F}_{2}(0))
and
\big\lvert\{j:T_{i,j}\leq t\}\big\rvert
the cardinality of the corresponding set. Moreover, in the third step we have applied that the counting processes
N_{i}(t)
,
t\in\mathcal{T},
are
\mathcal{F}_{2}(0)
-measurable, whereas the values of
G_{i,j}
and the filtration
\mathcal{F}_{2}(0)
are independent for all
j=1,\ldots,n_{i}
and
i=1,\ldots,n
. Additionally, in the fourth step we used that
G_{i,1},\ldots,G_{i,n_{i}}
are identically distributed with zero mean, unit variance and finite fourth moment for all
i=1,\ldots,n
.

3.

The martingale property is valid, as

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\big(\int_{0}^{t}(G_{i}^{2}(u)-1)dN_{i}(u)|\mathcal{F}_{2}(s)\big)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\big(\int_{0}^{s}(G_{i}^{2}(u)-1)dN_{i}(u)+\int_{s}^{t}(G_{i}^{2}(u)-1)dN_{i}(u)|\mathcal{F}_{2}(s)\big)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{s}(G_{i}^{2}(u)-1)dN_{i}(u)+\int_{s}^{t}\big(\mathbb{E}(G_{i}^{2}(u))-1\big)dN_{i}(u)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{s}(G_{i}^{2}(u)-1)dN_{i}(u),

where in the second step we have used that the counting process
N_{i}(t)
is
\mathcal{F}_{2}(0)\subset\mathcal{F}_{2}(t)
-measurable for
t\in\mathcal{T}
,
i=1,\ldots,n
. Furthermore, for a jump at
u\leq s
, the multiplier process
G_{i}(u)
is
\mathcal{F}_{2}(s)
-measurable, and,
if
u
is greater than or equal to the earliest jump time point, say
T_{i}(s^{+})
, of process
N_{i}
in
(s,\tau]
,
the values of
G_{i}(u)
and the filtration
\mathcal{F}_{2}(s)
are independent,
i=1,\ldots,n
. In the third step we used that the multiplier processes
G_{i}(t)
,
t\in\mathcal{T}
, have zero mean and unit variance,
i=1,\ldots,n
.

In conclusion,
\textbf{Q}_{n}
is a vector of local square integrable martingales.

Next, we wish to show that
\textbf{Q}_{n}(t)
converges to zero in probability, as
n\rightarrow\infty
. For this we apply Lenglart’s inequality and consider the predictable covariation process
\langle\textbf{Q}_{n}\rangle(\tau)
of the martingale
\textbf{Q}_{n}
at
\tau

\displaystyle\langle\textbf{Q}_{n}\rangle(\tau)

\displaystyle=\Big\langle\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\int_{0}^{\cdot}\text{vec}(\textbf{h}_{n,i}(u,\hat{\bm{\beta}}_{n})^{\otimes 2})(G_{i}^{2}(u)-1)dN_{i}(u)\Big\rangle(\tau)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n^{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\int_{0}^{\tau}\text{vec}(\textbf{h}_{n,i}(u,\hat{\bm{\beta}}_{n})^{\otimes 2})^{\otimes 2}d\Big\langle\int_{0}^{\cdot}(G_{i}^{2}(v)-1)dN_{i}(v)\Big\rangle(u)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n^{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\int_{0}^{\tau}\text{vec}(\textbf{h}_{n,i}(u,\hat{\bm{\beta}}_{n})^{\otimes 2})^{\otimes 2}(\mathbb{E}(G_{i}^{4}(u))-1)dN_{i}(u),

where in the second step we have used that

\displaystyle d\Big\langle\int_{0}^{\cdot}(G_{i}^{2}(u)-1)dN_{i}(u),\int_{0}^{\cdot}(G_{l}^{2}(u)-1)dN_{l}(u)\Big\rangle(t)

\displaystyle=\text{Cov}\big((G_{i}^{2}(t)-1)dN_{i}(t),(G_{l}^{2}(t)-1)dN_{l}(t)|\mathcal{F}_{t\text{-}}\big)

\displaystyle=\text{Cov}\big(G_{i}^{2}(t),G_{l}^{2}(t)\big)dN_{i}(t)dN_{l}(t)

\displaystyle=0,

because
G_{1}(t),\ldots,G_{n}(t)
,
t\in\mathcal{T}
, are pairwise independent and no two counting processes jump simultaneously. The third step holds due to

\displaystyle d\Big\langle\int_{0}^{\cdot}(G_{i}^{2}(u)-1)dN_{i}(u)\Big\rangle(t)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\big([(G_{i}^{2}(t)-1)dN_{i}(t)]^{2}|\mathcal{F}_{t\text{-}}\big)

\displaystyle=\big(\mathbb{E}(G_{i}^{4}(t))-2\mathbb{E}(G_{i}(t)^{2})+1\big)dN_{i}(t)

\displaystyle=\big(\mathbb{E}(G_{i}^{4}(t))-1\big)dN_{i}(t).

We continue by stating that

\displaystyle\langle\textbf{Q}_{n}\rangle(\tau)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n^{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\int_{0}^{\tau}\text{vec}(\tilde{\textbf{h}}_{i}(u,\bm{\beta}_{0})^{\otimes 2})^{\otimes 2}(\mathbb{E}(G_{i}^{4}(u))-1)dN_{i}(u)

(I.53)

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{n^{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\int_{0}^{\tau}[\text{vec}(\textbf{h}_{n,i}(u,\hat{\bm{\beta}}_{n})^{\otimes 2})^{\otimes 2}-\text{vec}(\tilde{\textbf{h}}_{i}(u,\bm{\beta}_{0})^{\otimes 2})^{\otimes 2}](\mathbb{E}(G_{i}^{4}(u))-1)dN_{i}(u).

For the first term on the right-hand side we have

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\frac{1}{n^{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\int_{0}^{\tau}\text{vec}(\tilde{\textbf{h}}_{i}(u,\bm{\beta}_{0})^{\otimes 2})^{\otimes 2}(\mathbb{E}(G_{i}^{4}(u))-1)dN_{i}(u)\\
&\leq\frac{1}{n}\sup_{i\in\{1,\ldots,n\},t\in\mathcal{T}}\lVert\tilde{\textbf{h}}_{i}(t,\bm{\beta}_{0})\rVert_{\infty}^{4}(\mathbb{E}(G_{1,1}^{4})-1)\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}N_{i}(\tau)=o_{p}(1),\text{ as }n\rightarrow\infty,\end{split}

(I.54)

since
\mathbb{E}(G_{1,1}^{4})<\infty
according to
I.2.1

(ii)
, and
\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}N_{i}(\tau)=O_{p}(1)
, as was derived at the beginning of the proof of
I.2.4
.
Additionally, for the second term on the right-hand side we find

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\frac{1}{n^{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\int_{0}^{\tau}[\text{vec}(\textbf{h}_{n,i}(u,\hat{\bm{\beta}}_{n})^{\otimes 2})^{\otimes 2}-\text{vec}(\tilde{\textbf{h}}_{i}(u,\bm{\beta}_{0})^{\otimes 2})^{\otimes 2}](\mathbb{E}(G_{i}^{4}(u))-1)dN_{i}(u)\\
&\leq\frac{1}{n}\sup_{i\in\{1,\ldots,n\},t\in\mathcal{T}}\lVert\text{vec}(\textbf{h}_{n,i}(t,\hat{\bm{\beta}}_{n})^{\otimes 2})^{\otimes 2}-\text{vec}(\tilde{\textbf{h}}_{i}(t,\bm{\beta}_{0})^{\otimes 2})^{\otimes 2}\rVert_{\infty}\\
&\quad\cdot(\mathbb{E}(G_{1,1}^{4})-1)\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}N_{i}(\tau)\\
&\leq\ \rVert\textbf{h}_{n,i}(t,\hat{\bm{\beta}}_{n})\lVert_{\infty}^{2}\Big[\lVert\textbf{h}_{n,i}(t,\hat{\bm{\beta}}_{n})-\tilde{\textbf{h}}_{i}(t,{\bm{\beta}_{0}})\rVert_{\infty}\lVert\textbf{h}_{n,i}(t,\hat{\bm{\beta}}_{n})\rVert_{\infty}\\
&{}\quad+\lVert\tilde{\textbf{h}}_{i}(t,{\bm{\beta}_{0}})\rVert_{\infty}\lVert\textbf{h}_{n,i}(t,\hat{\bm{\beta}}_{n})-\tilde{\textbf{h}}_{i}(t,{\bm{\beta}_{0}})\rVert_{\infty}\Big]\\
&{}\quad+\lVert\tilde{\textbf{h}}_{i}(t,{\bm{\beta}_{0}})\rVert_{\infty}^{2}\Big[\lVert\textbf{h}_{n,i}(t,\hat{\bm{\beta}}_{n})-\tilde{\textbf{h}}_{i}(t,{\bm{\beta}_{0}})\rVert_{\infty}\lVert\textbf{h}_{n,i}(t,\hat{\bm{\beta}}_{n})\rVert_{\infty}\\
&{}\quad+\lVert\tilde{\textbf{h}}_{i}(t,{\bm{\beta}_{0}})\rVert_{\infty}\lVert\textbf{h}_{n,i}(t,\hat{\bm{\beta}}_{n})-\tilde{\textbf{h}}_{i}(t,{\bm{\beta}_{0}})\rVert_{\infty}\Big]\Big)\\
&\quad\cdot\frac{1}{n}(\mathbb{E}(G_{1,1}^{4})-1)\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}N_{i}(\tau)\\
&=o_{p}(1),\text{ as }n\rightarrow\infty,\end{split}

(I.55)

where we used
\mathbb{E}(G_{1,1}^{4})<\infty
,
\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}N_{i}(\tau)=O_{p}(1)
,
\rVert\textbf{h}_{n,i}(t,\hat{\bm{\beta}}_{n})\lVert_{\infty}<\infty
,
I.2.1

(i)
,
(ii)
, and (
I.52
) in combination with the triangle inequality. In particular, the terms in brackets vanish asymptotically, as
n\rightarrow\infty
.
Combining (
I.53
), (
I.54
) and (
I.55
), we get
\langle\textbf{Q}_{n}\rangle(\tau)=o_{p}(1)
, as
n\rightarrow\infty
, and with Lenglart’s inequality it follows that

\textbf{Q}_{n}(t)=\text{vec}\big([\textbf{D}_{n,h}^{*}](t)-\langle\textbf{D}_{n,h}^{*}\rangle(t)\big)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathbb{P}}}{{\longrightarrow}}0,\text{ as }n\rightarrow\infty,\text{ for all }t\in\mathcal{T}.

In combination with
I.3.5
, we have
[\textbf{D}_{n,h}^{*}](t)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathbb{P}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\textbf{V}_{\tilde{h}}(t)
, as
n\to\infty
, for all
t\in\mathcal{T}
. This completes the proof of
I.3.7
.
\blacksquare

Proof of
I.3.8
.

Recall from (
I.18
) that
\textbf{B}_{n}^{*}(t)=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\int_{0}^{t}\textnormal{D}\textbf{k}_{n,i}(u,\hat{\bm{\beta}}_{n})\big(G_{i}(u)+1\big)dN_{i}(u)
. Then, we have

\displaystyle\begin{split}&{}\sup_{t\in\mathcal{T}}\lVert\textbf{B}^{*}_{n}(t)-\textbf{B}(t)\rVert\\
&{}\leq\sup_{t\in\mathcal{T}}\Big\lVert\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\int_{0}^{t}[\textnormal{D}\textbf{k}_{n,i}(u,\bm{\beta}_{0})-\tilde{\textbf{K}}_{i}(u,\bm{\beta}_{0})](G_{i}(u)+1)dN_{i}(u)\Big\rVert\\
&{}\quad+\sup_{t\in\mathcal{T}}\Big\lVert\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\int_{0}^{t}\tilde{\textbf{K}}_{i}(u,\bm{\beta}_{0})(G_{i}(u)+1)dN_{i}(u)-\textbf{B}(t)\Big\rVert\\
&{}\leq\sup_{t\in\mathcal{T}}\Big\lVert\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\int_{0}^{t}[\textnormal{D}\textbf{k}_{n,i}(u,\bm{\beta}_{0})-\tilde{\textbf{K}}_{i}(u,\bm{\beta}_{0})](G_{i}(u)+1)dN_{i}(u)\Big\rVert\\
&{}\quad+\sup_{t\in\mathcal{T}}\Big\lVert\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\int_{0}^{t}\tilde{\textbf{K}}_{i}(u,\bm{\beta}_{0})G_{i}(u)dN_{i}(u)\Big\rVert\\
&{}\quad+\sup_{t\in\mathcal{T}}\Big\lVert\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\int_{0}^{t}\tilde{\textbf{K}}_{i}(u,\bm{\beta}_{0})dN_{i}(u)-\textbf{B}(t)\Big\rVert.\end{split}

(I.56)

We consider the second term on the right-hand side of the second step of (
I.56
) first. According to
I.3.2
with
h_{n,i}(t,\hat{{\beta}}_{n})\equiv 1
,
\int_{0}^{t}G_{i}(u)\,dN_{i}(u)
is a square integrable martingale w.r.t.
\mathcal{F}_{2}
. Moreover, it holds that
\int_{0}^{\tau}\lvert G_{i}(u)\,dN_{i}(u)\rvert\leq\max_{j=1,\ldots,n_{i}}\lvert G_{i,j}\rvert N_{i}(\tau)<\infty
almost surely, as the maximum is taken over finitely many almost surely finite random variables. Thus, the martingale is also of finite variation. Due to
I.2.3

(ii)
and with Theorem II.3.1. of
Andersen et al. 1993
, it follows that
\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\int_{0}^{t}\tilde{\textbf{K}}_{i}(u,\bm{\beta}_{0})G_{i}(u)dN_{i}(u)
is a local square integrable martingale w.r.t.
\mathcal{F}_{2}
. Furthermore, its predictable covariation process at
\tau
is given by

\displaystyle\begin{split}&{}\Big\langle\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\int_{0}^{\cdot}\tilde{\textbf{K}}_{i}(u,\bm{\beta}_{0})G_{i}(u)dN_{i}(u)\Big\rangle(\tau)\\
&{}=\frac{1}{n^{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\Big\langle\int_{0}^{\cdot}\tilde{\textbf{K}}_{i}(u,\bm{\beta}_{0})G_{i}(u)dN_{i}(u),\int_{0}^{\cdot}\tilde{\textbf{K}}_{j}(u,\bm{\beta}_{0})G_{j}(u)dN_{j}(u)\Big\rangle(\tau)\\
&{}=\frac{1}{n^{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\int_{0}^{\tau}\tilde{\textbf{K}}_{i}(u,\bm{\beta}_{0})\,d\Big\langle\int_{0}^{\cdot}G_{i}(s)dN_{i}(s),\int_{0}^{\cdot}G_{j}(s)dN_{j}(s)\Big\rangle(u)\,\tilde{\textbf{K}}_{j}(u,\bm{\beta}_{0})^{\top}\\
&{}=\frac{1}{n^{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\int_{0}^{\tau}\tilde{\textbf{K}}_{i}(u,\bm{\beta}_{0})^{\otimes 2}\,dN_{i}(u),\end{split}

(I.57)

because
\langle\int_{0}^{\cdot}G_{i}(s)dN_{i}(s),\int_{0}^{\cdot}G_{j}(s)dN_{j}(s)\rangle(u)=N_{i}(u)
, for
i=j
, and zero otherwise, according to
I.3.2
. Additionally, in the second step of (
I.57
) the aforementioned Theorem II.3.1. has been used. For the remaining part of this proof we use unconditional convergence in probability instead of conditionally on
\mathcal{F}_{2}(0)
, because due to Fact 1 of the supplement of
Dobler et al. 2019
these two types of convergence are equivalent.
We wish to show that the last term on the right-hand side of (
I.57
) converges to zero in probability, as
n\rightarrow\infty
. For this, we bound that term from above by
\frac{1}{n}\sup_{i\in\{1,\ldots,n\},t\in\mathcal{T}}\lVert\tilde{\textbf{K}}_{i}(t,\bm{\beta}_{0})\rVert_{\infty}^{2}\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}N_{i}(\tau)
. Recall that
\sup_{i\in\{1,\ldots,n\},t\in\mathcal{T}}\lVert\tilde{\textbf{K}}_{i}(t,\bm{\beta}_{0})\rVert_{\infty}^{2}<\infty
, by
I.2.3

(ii)
, and
\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}N_{i}(\tau)=O_{p}(1)
, by the integrability of
\Lambda_{i}(\tau,\mathbf{{\beta}}_{0})
and
I.2.1

(iii)
, as stated at the beginning of
I.2.4
. Hence, the predictable covariation process of
\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\int_{0}^{t}\tilde{\textbf{K}}_{i}(u,\bm{\beta}_{0})G_{i}(u)dN_{i}(u)
at
\tau
converges to zero in probability, as
n\rightarrow\infty
. With Lenglart’s inequality it follows that the corresponding martingale converges to zero in probability, as
n\rightarrow\infty
, for all
t\in\mathcal{T}
. In other words, the second term on the right-hand side of the second step of (
I.56
) vanishes asymptotically.

Next, we consider the first term on the right-hand side of the second step of (
I.56
). For this term we get

\displaystyle{}\sup_{t\in\mathcal{T}}\lVert\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\int_{0}^{t}[\textnormal{D}\textbf{k}_{n,i}(u,\bm{\beta}_{0})-\tilde{\textbf{K}}_{i}(u,\bm{\beta}_{0})](G_{i}(u)+1)dN_{i}(u)\rVert

\displaystyle{}\leq\sup_{i\in\{1,\ldots,n\},t\in\mathcal{T}}\lVert\textnormal{D}\textbf{k}_{n,i}(t,\hat{\bm{\beta}})-\tilde{\textbf{K}}_{i}(t,\bm{\beta}_{0})\rVert\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\int_{0}^{\tau}|G_{i}(u)+1\rvert\,dN_{i}(u).

According to
I.2.3

(i)
, the first term on the right-hand side of the inequality above converges to zero in probability, as
n\rightarrow\infty
. We now address the corresponding second term, which can be rewritten as
\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n_{i}}\lvert G_{i,j}+1\rvert
. Furthermore, we have

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathbb{E}\Big(\sum_{j=1}^{n_{i}}\rvert G_{i,j}+1\rvert\Big)&{}=\mathbb{E}\Big(\mathbb{E}\Big(\sum_{j=1}^{n_{i}}\rvert G_{i,j}+1\rvert|\mathcal{F}_{2}(0)\Big)\Big)\\
&{}=\mathbb{E}\Big(\sum_{j=1}^{n_{i}}\mathbb{E}(\rvert G_{i,j}+1\rvert)\Big)\\
&{}\leq 2\mathbb{E}(N_{i}(\tau))<\infty,\end{split}

(I.58)

where
in the second step we have used that
N_{i}(t)
with
N_{i}(\tau)=n_{i}
is
\mathcal{F}_{2}(0)
-measurable and
G_{i}(t)
,
t\in\mathcal{T}
, is independent of
\mathcal{F}_{2}(0)
. Additionally, in the last step of (
I.58
) we employed that
\text{Var}(\lvert G_{i,j}\rvert)=\mathbb{E}(G_{i,j}^{2})-\mathbb{E}(\lvert G_{i,j}\rvert)^{2}\geq 0
and
\mathbb{E}(G_{i,j}^{2})=1
implies
\mathbb{E}(\lvert G_{i,j}\rvert)\leq 1
. As the pairs
(G_{i}(t),N_{i}(t))
are pairwise independent and identically distributed, it follows with (
I.58
) and the law of large numbers that
\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n_{i}}\lvert G_{i,j}+1\rvert\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathbb{P}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathbb{E}(\sum_{j=1}^{n_{1}}\lvert G_{1,j}+1\rvert)
, as
n\rightarrow\infty
.
Finally, we conclude that
\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\int_{0}^{\tau}|G_{i}(u)+1\rvert\,dN_{i}(u)=O_{p}(1)
, which is why also the first term on the right-hand side of the second step of (
I.56
) converges to zero in probability, as
n\rightarrow\infty
. It is only left to consider the third term on the right-hand side of the second step of (
I.56
). In fact, we have already shown in the proof of
I.2.4
that this term converges to zero in probability, as
n\rightarrow\infty
.
Thus, we have proven that all three terms of (
I.56
) converge to zero in probability, as
n\rightarrow\infty
, which completes the proof of
I.3.8
.

\blacksquare

Proof of
I.3.10
.

We aim to derive the weak limit of the term
\textbf{D}^{*}_{n,k}+\textbf{B}^{*}_{n}\textbf{C}^{*}_{n}\textbf{D}^{*}_{n,g}(\tau)
, as
n\rightarrow\infty
, where
\textbf{D}^{*}_{n,k}
and
\textbf{D}^{*}_{n,g}
are vector-valued stochastic processes,
\textbf{B}^{*}_{n}
is a matrix-valued stochastic process and
\textbf{C}^{*}_{n}
is a random matrix. Recall the notation introduced in the proof of
I.3.6
regarding the product probability space
(\Omega_{1},\mathcal{A}_{1},\mathbb{P}_{1})~\otimes~(\Omega_{2},\mathcal{A}_{2},\mathbb{P}_{2})
, the convergence in law w.r.t
\mathbb{P}_{2}
,
\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb{P}_{2}}}}{{\longrightarrow}}
, and
\cdot|\mathcal{F}_{2}(0)(\omega)
. According to
I.3.6
,
we have, conditionally on
\mathcal{F}_{2}(0)
,

({\textbf{D}^{*}_{n,k}}^{\top},{\textbf{D}^{*}_{n,g}}^{\top})^{\top}=\textbf{D}^{*}_{n,h}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb{P}_{2}}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\textbf{D}_{\tilde{h}}
, in
(D(\mathcal{T}))^{p+b}
, as
n\rightarrow\infty,
in
\mathbb{P}_{1}
-probability, where
\textbf{D}_{\tilde{h}}
is given in
I.2.6
. Thus, for every subsequence
n_{1}
of
n
there exists a further subsequence
n_{2}
such that

\displaystyle\textbf{D}^{*}_{n_{2},h}|\mathcal{F}_{2}(0)(\omega)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb{P}_{2}}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\textbf{D}_{\tilde{h}},\text{ in }(D(\mathcal{T}))^{p+b},\text{ as }n\rightarrow\infty,

(I.59)

for
\mathbb{P}_{1}
-almost all
\omega\in\Omega_{1}
. Moreover, with
I.3.8
it follows that, conditionally on
\mathcal{F}_{2}(0)
,
